**Muammar Muhammad Abu Minyar al-Gaddafi**[^cite_note-8][^cite_note-15] (c. 1942 – 20 October 2011) was a Libyan military officer, [revolutionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutionary), politician, and political theorist who ruled [Libya](17633.md) from 1969 until [his overthrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Tripoli_\(2011\)) by Libyan [rebel forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Gaddafi_forces) in 2011 during the [First Libyan Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libyan_Civil_War_\(2011\)). He came to power through a bloodless [military coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1969_Libyan_revolution), first becoming [Revolutionary Chairman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutionary_Chairman_of_the_Libyan_Arab_Republic) of the [Libyan Arab Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libyan_Arab_Republic) from 1969 to 1977, [Secretary General of the General People's Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_General_of_the_General_People's_Congress) from 1977 to 1979, and then the [Brotherly Leader](32860582.md) of the [Great Socialist People's Libyan Arab Jamahiriya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Socialist_People's_Libyan_Arab_Jamahiriya) from 1979 to 2011. Initially ideologically committed to [Arab nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_nationalism) and [Arab socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_socialism), Gaddafi later ruled according to his own [Third International Theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_International_Theory).

Gaddafi became an Arab nationalist while at school in [Sabha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabha,_Libya), later enrolling in the [Royal Military Academy, Benghazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benghazi_Military_University_Academy). He founded a revolutionary group known as the [Free Officers movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Officers_movement_\(Libya\)) which deposed the [Western](21208200.md)-backed [Senussi monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Libya) of [Idris I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idris_of_Libya) in a [1969 coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1969_Libyan_revolution). Gaddafi converted Libya into a republic governed by his [Revolutionary Command Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libyan_Revolutionary_Command_Council). [Ruling by decree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rule_by_decree), he [deported Libya's Italian population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1970_expulsion_of_Italians_from_Libya) and ejected its Western military bases.&#x20;

He strengthened ties to Arab nationalist governments and unsuccessfully advocated [pan-Arab political union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Arabism). An [Islamic modernist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_modernism), he introduced [Sharia law](28840.md) and promoted [Islamic socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_socialism). He nationalized the oil industry and used the increasing state revenues to bolster the military, [fund foreign revolutionaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Libya_under_Muammar_Gaddafi#Support_for_rebel_and_paramilitary_groups), and implement social programs emphasizing housebuilding, healthcare and education projects. In 1973, he initiated a "[Popular Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_Revolution_in_Libya)" with the formation of [Basic People's Congresses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basic_People's_Congress_\(political\)), presented as a system of [direct democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direct_democracy), but retained personal control over major decisions. He outlined his Third International Theory that year in *[The Green Book](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Green_Book_\(Muammar_Gaddafi\))*.

In 1977, Gaddafi transformed Libya into a new [socialist state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_state) called a *[Jamahiriya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Libya_under_Muammar_Gaddafi#Great_Socialist_People's_Libyan_Arab_Jamahiriya_\(1977%E2%80%932011\))* ("state of the masses"). He officially adopted a symbolic role in governance but remained head of both the military and the Revolutionary Committees responsible for policing and suppressing dissent. During the 1970s and 1980s, Libya's unsuccessful border conflicts with [Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian%E2%80%93Libyan_War) and [Chad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chadian%E2%80%93Libyan_conflict), support for foreign militants and alleged responsibility for bombings of [Pan Am Flight 103](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan_Am_Flight_103) and [UTA Flight 772](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UTA_Flight_772) left it increasingly isolated on the world stage.

A particularly hostile relationship developed with Israel, the United States and the United Kingdom, resulting in the [1986 U.S. bombing of Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_United_States_bombing_of_Libya) and United Nations–imposed economic sanctions. From 1999, Gaddafi shunned pan-Arabism, and encouraged [pan-Africanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Africanism) and rapprochement with Western nations; he was [Chairperson of the African Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chairperson_of_the_African_Union) from 2009 to 2010. Amid the 2011 [Arab Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Spring), protests against widespread corruption and unemployment broke out in eastern Libya. The situation descended into [civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libyan_civil_war_\(2011\)), in which [NATO](21133.md) [intervened militarily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_military_intervention_in_Libya) on the side of the anti-Gaddafist [National Transitional Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Transitional_Council) (NTC). Gaddafi's government was overthrown; he retreated to Sirte only to be captured, tortured [and killed by NTC militants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killing_of_Muammar_Gaddafi).

A highly divisive figure, Gaddafi dominated Libya's politics for four decades and was the subject of a pervasive [cult of personality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cult_of_personality). He was decorated with various awards and praised for his [anti-imperialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-imperialist) stance, support for Arab—and then African—unity, as well as for significant development to the country after the discovery of oil reserves. Conversely, many Libyans strongly opposed Gaddafi's social and economic reforms; he was accused of various human rights violations. He was condemned by many as a dictator whose [authoritarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarianism) administration systematically [violated human rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_violations_during_the_Gaddafi_regime) and financed [terrorism](30636.md) in the region and abroad.

## Early life and career

Main article: [Early life and career of Muammar Gaddafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_life_and_career_of_Muammar_Gaddafi)

Muammar Gaddafi was born near [Qasr Abu Hadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qasr_Abu_Hadi), a rural area outside the town of [Sirte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sirte) in the deserts of [Tripolitania](664792.md), Italian western Libya.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198733Kawczynski20119St._John2012135-16] His family came from a small, relatively uninfluential tribe called the [Qadhadhfa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qadhadhfa),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198658BlundyLycett198733Simons1996170Kawczynski20119-17] who were of Arab [Ashraf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashraf) heritage, from the lineage of [Musa al-Kazim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musa_al-Kazim).[^cite_note-18] According to later claims, Gaddafi's paternal grandfather, Abdessalam Bouminyar, was killed by the Italian Army during the [Italian invasion of 1911](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italo-Turkish_War).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBianco19754BlundyLycett198737Kawczynski20114-19] Subsequently, moving to nearby Sirte to attend elementary school, he progressed through six grades in four years.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBianco19755–6,_8–9BlundyLycett198739Kawczynski201110St._John2012136-20] Growing up, Gaddafi witnessed significant events severely disturb the [Arab world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_world), including the [1948 Arab–Israeli War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Arab%E2%80%93Israeli_War), the [Egyptian Revolution of 1952](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Revolution_of_1952), the [Suez Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suez_Crisis) of 1956, and the short-lived existence of the [United Arab Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Arab_Republic) (UAR) between 1958 and 1961.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012136-21] In October 1961, he led a demonstration protesting against [Syria](7515849.md)'s secession from the UAR and raised funds to send cables of support to Nasser. Twenty students were arrested as a result of the disorder. Gaddafi and his companions also broke windows in a local hotel that was [accused of serving alcohol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_dietary_laws#Intoxicants). To punish Gaddafi, the authorities expelled him and his family from Sabha.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198658BlundyLycett198742–43Simons1996171–172Kawczynski201111St._John2012136-22]

Gaddafi moved to [Misrata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misrata), there attending Misrata Secondary School.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198658BlundyLycett198744Simons1996172Kawczynski201111St._John2012137-23] He graduated from the [Benghazi Military College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benghazi_Military_University_Academy) in 1965.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012138-24] In 1966, he went to the [United Kingdom](31717.md) for further military training.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198749–50Simons1996174Kawczynski201113St._John2012138-25]

## Libyan Arab Republic

Further information: [History of Libya under Muammar Gaddafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Libya_under_Muammar_Gaddafi)

### Coup d'état: 1969

Main article: [1969 Libyan revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1969_Libyan_revolution)

> People of Libya! In response to your own will, fulfilling your most heartfelt wishes, answering your most incessant demands for change and regeneration, and your longing to strive towards these ends: listening to your incitement to rebel, your armed forces have undertaken the overthrow of the corrupt regime, the stench of which has sickened and horrified us all. At a single blow our gallant army has toppled these idols and has destroyed their images. By a single stroke it has lightened the long dark night in which the Turkish domination was followed first by Italian rule, then by this reactionary and decadent regime which was no more than a hotbed of extortion, faction, treachery and treason.

— Gaddafi's radio speech after seizing power, 1969

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198654Simons1996178–179-26]

Idris' government was increasingly unpopular by the late 1960s, having exacerbated Libya's traditional regional and tribal divisions by [centralizing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_state) the country's [federal system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalism) to take advantage of the country's oil wealth.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198614BlundyLycett198752Kawczynski201115–16-27] Corruption and entrenched patronage systems were widespread throughout the oil industry.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198751Kawczynski2011136-28] Arab nationalism was increasingly popular, and protests flared up after Egypt's 1967 defeat in the [Six-Day War](29328.md) with Israel; Idris' administration was seen as pro-Israeli due to its alliance with the West.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESimons1996175Vandewalle200670Kawczynski201116–17-29] Anti-Western riots broke out in [Tripoli](31232.md) and Benghazi, while Libyan workers shut down oil terminals in solidarity with Egypt.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESimons1996175Vandewalle200670Kawczynski201116–17-29] By 1969, the US [Central Intelligence Agency](5183633.md) (CIA) was expecting segments of Libya's armed forces to launch a coup. Although claims have been made that they knew of Gaddafi's [Free Officers Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Officers_Movement_\(Libya\)), they have since claimed ignorance, stating that they were instead monitoring the Black Boots revolutionary group of Abdul Aziz Shalhi, Idris' *de facto* chief of staff.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198753Kawczynski201119St._John2012139–140-30] Shalhi and his brother Omar were the sons of Idris' former chief advisor Ibrahim Shalhi, who had been murdered by [Queen Fatima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatimah_el-Sharif)'s nephew in 1954.[^cite_note-31] After their father's assassination, they became the favorites of Idris.[^cite_note-32]

In mid-1969, Idris spent the summer in Turkey and Greece amid widespread rumors of an abdication or a British-backed coup by the Shalhi brothers on 5 September.[^cite_note-\:7-33] Gaddafi's Free Officers, recognizing this as their last chance to preempt the Shelhis in overthrowing the monarchy, initiated "Operation Jerusalem".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198652Kawczynski201118-34] If Gaddafi's Free Officers had not preempted the Shelhis, they would have almost certainly been defeated by the combined forces of Abdul Aziz Shelhi, the deputy commander of Libya's army, and the prominent families in [Cyrenaica](75451.md) that supported the Shelhi family.[^cite_note-35] On 1 September, Gaddafi's Free Officers occupied airports, police depots, radio stations, and government offices in Tripoli and Benghazi. Gaddafi took control of the Berka barracks in Benghazi, while [Umar Muhayshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umar_Muhayshi) occupied Tripoli barracks and Jalloud seized the city's anti-aircraft batteries. [Khweldi Hameidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khweldi_Hameidi) took over the Tripoli radio station and arrested crown prince [Sayyid Hasan ar-Rida al-Mahdi as-Sanussi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasan_as-Senussi), forcing him to relinquish his claim to the throne.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198614BlundyLycett198757–59Simons1996177–178Kawczynski201118-36][^cite_note-37] They met no serious resistance and wielded little violence against the monarchists.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESimons1996178Kawczynski201118-38]

Once Gaddafi removed the government, he announced the foundation of the [Libyan Arab Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Libya_under_Muammar_Gaddafi).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198655Harris198615Simons1996179-39] Addressing the populace by radio, he proclaimed an end to the "reactionary and corrupt" regime, "the stench of which has sickened and horrified us all".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198654Harris198614BlundyLycett198759–60Kawczynski201118-40] Due to the coup's bloodless nature, it was initially labelled the "White Revolution", although was later renamed the "One September Revolution" after its date.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012134-41] Gaddafi insisted that the Free Officers' coup represented a revolution, marking the start of widespread change in the socio-economic and political nature of Libya.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John1983472Bearman198656St._John2012159-42] He proclaimed that the revolution meant "freedom, socialism, and unity", and soon implemented measures to achieve this.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198662Harris198615BlundyLycett198764St._John2012148-43]

### Consolidating leadership: 1969–1973

The 12-member central committee of the Free Officers proclaimed themselves the [Revolutionary Command Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libyan_Revolutionary_Command_Council) (RCC), the government of the new republic.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198763Vandewalle2008b9St._John2012134-44] Gaddafi became chairman, and therefore *de facto* head of state, also appointing himself [colonel](199023.md) and becoming commander-in-chief of the armed forces.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198615BlundyLycett198764St._John2012134-45] Jalloud became Prime Minister,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198791–92-46] while a civilian Council of Ministers headed by Sulaiman Maghribi was founded to implement RCC policy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198617BlundyLycett198763-47] Libya's administrative capital was moved from [al-Beida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayda,_Libya) to Tripoli.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198671-48]



The flag of republican Libya used by Gaddafi's government from 1969 to 1972

Although theoretically a consensus-building [collegial body](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collegial_body), Gaddafi dominated the RCC.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012134-41] Some of the others attempted to constrain what they saw as his excesses.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski201120-49] Gaddafi remained the government's public face, with the identities of the other RCC members only publicly revealed on 10 January 1970.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle200679Vandewalle2008b9St._John2012134-50] All were young men from lower-class backgrounds without university degrees, which distinguished them from the wealthy, educated conservatives who previously governed the country.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198638Vandewalle200679Vandewalle2008b10Kawczynski201120-51]

The coup completed, the RCC proceeded with consolidating power and modernizing the country.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012134-41] They purged monarchists and members of Idris' [Senussi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senussi) clan from Libya's political world and armed forces; Gaddafi believed them opposed to the will of the Libyan people.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle2008b11Kawczynski201121–23-52] [People's Courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libyan_People's_Court) were founded to try various monarchist politicians and journalists, many of whom were imprisoned, although none executed. Idris was sentenced to execution *[in absentia](582826.md)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198671Harris198616BlundyLycett198762-53] Three months after Gaddafi came to power, the army minister and interior minister, both from the eastern [Barqa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barqa) region, tried to overthrow him in a failed [coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1969_Libyan_coup_attempt).[^cite_note-54] In 1970, Idris' great-nephew [Ahmed al-Senussi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmed_al-Senussi) attempted another coup against Gaddafi; the [monarchist plot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Prince_conspiracy) was foiled in August and Ahmed was sentenced to death (commuted in 1988 and pardoned by Gaddafi in 2001).[^cite_note-55][^cite_note-56]

In May 1970, the Revolutionary Intellectuals Seminar was held to bring intellectuals in line with the revolution,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198617-57] while that year's Legislative Review and Amendment introduced *[sharia](28840.md)* into the legal system.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198616-58] [Ruling by decree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rule_by_decree), the RCC maintained the monarchy's ban on political parties, banned trade unions, and in 1972 outlawed workers' strikes and suspended newspapers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198617BlundyLycett198763–64Vandewalle2008b11St._John2012153-59] In September 1971, Gaddafi resigned, claiming dissatisfaction with the pace of reform, but returned to his position within a month.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198791–92-46] In July 1972, amid widespread speculation that Gaddafi had been ousted or jailed by his political opponents, a new 18-man cabinet was formed with only two, Jalloud and [Abdel Moneim al-Houni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdel_Moneim_al-Houni), being military men; the rest were civilian [technocrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technocracy) per Gaddafi's insistence.[^cite_note-60][^cite_note-61] In February 1973, Gaddafi resigned again, once more returning the next month.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198785-62]

#### Economic and social reform



Gaddafi at an Arab summit in Libya in 1969, shortly after the September Revolution that toppled [King Idris I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idris_of_Libya). Gaddafi sits in military uniform in the middle, surrounded by Egyptian President [Gamal Abdel Nasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamal_Abdel_Nasser) (left) and Syrian President [Nureddin al-Atassi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nureddin_al-Atassi) (right).

The RCC's early economic policy has been characterized as being [state capitalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_capitalism) in orientation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986124-63] Many initiatives were established to aid entrepreneurs and develop a Libyan bourgeoisie.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986123-64] Seeking to expand cultivatable acreage, in September 1969 the government launched a "Green Revolution" to increase agricultural productivity and lessen Libyan reliance on imported food.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986128-65] They hoped to make Libya self-sufficient in food production.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEl-Khawas198434-66] All land expropriated from Italian settlers or unused was repossessed and redistributed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986129-67] Irrigation systems were established along the northern coastline and various inland oases.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986130–132-68] Production costs often surpassed produce value, keeping production in deficit and relying on state subsidies.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986132-69]

With [crude oil](23195.md) as the country's primary export, Gaddafi sought to improve Libya's oil sector.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198766–67St._John2012145–146-70] In October 1969, he proclaimed the current trade terms unfair, benefiting foreign corporations more than the Libyan state, and threatened to decrease production. In December, Jalloud successfully increased the price of Libyan oil.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198680–88BlundyLycett198766–67St._John2012145–146-71] In 1970, other [OPEC](166346.md) states followed suit, leading to a global increase in the price of crude oil.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198766–67St._John2012145–146-70] The RCC followed with the Tripoli Agreement of 1971, in which they secured income tax, back-payments and better pricing from the oil corporations; these measures brought Libya an estimated \$1 billion in additional revenues in its first year.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle2008b15St._John2012147-72]

Increasing state control over the oil sector, the RCC began a program of [nationalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalization), starting with the expropriation of [British Petroleum](18998720.md)'s share of the British Petroleum-N.B. Hunt Sahir Field in December 1971.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198690BlundyLycett198768St._John2012147-73] In September 1973, all foreign oil producers in Libya saw 51 per cent of their operation nationalized.[^cite_note-Libya-74] Among the companies that were partially nationalized was [Armand Hammer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armand_Hammer)'s [Occidental Petroleum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occidental_Petroleum).[^cite_note-75][^cite_note-76] For Gaddafi, this was an essential step towards socialism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198691BlundyLycett198768St._John1987116St._John2012147-77] It proved an economic success; while gross domestic product had been \$3.8 billion in 1969, it had risen to \$13.7 billion in 1974, and \$24.5 billion in 1979.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett1987107-78] In turn, Libyan standards of life greatly improved over the first decade of Gaddafi's administration, and by 1979 the average per-capita income was at \$8,170, up from \$40 in 1951; above that of many industrialized countries like Italy and the UK.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett1987107-78] In 1969, the government ordered all foreign owned banks to close down or convert to joint-stock operations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEl-Khawas198428-79]



In 1971, Egypt's [Anwar Sadat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anwar_Sadat), Libya's Gaddafi and Syria's [Hafez al-Assad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafez_al-Assad) signed an agreement [to form a federal Union of Arab Republics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation_of_Arab_Republics). The agreement never materialized into a federal union between the three Arab states.

The RCC implemented social reform measures, adopting *sharia* as a basis.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198764Vandewalle2008b31Kawczynski201121St._John2012134-80] Consumption of alcohol was prohibited, night clubs and [Christian churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Libya) were shut down, traditional Libyan dress was encouraged, and Arabic was declared the only language permitted in official communications and on road signs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198672BlundyLycett198764Vandewalle2008b31Kawczynski201121St._John2012134-81] The RCC doubled the [minimum wage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minimum_wage), introduced statutory price controls, and implemented compulsory rent reductions of between 30 and 40 per cent.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198673-82] Gaddafi also wanted to combat the strict social restrictions imposed on women by the previous regime, establishing the [Revolutionary Women's Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutionary_Women's_Formation) to encourage reform.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986196-83] In 1970, a law was introduced affirming equality of the sexes and wage parity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986198-84] In 1971, Gaddafi sponsored the creation of a Libyan General Women's Federation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986197-85] In 1972, a law was passed criminalizing the marriage of any females under the age of sixteen and making women's consent a prerequisite for marriage.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986198-84] Gaddafi's regime created a wide range of educational and employment opportunities for women, although these primarily benefited a minority in the urban middle-classes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986198-84]



Gaddafi addresses students, 1970

Oil money funded social welfare programs, leading to housebuilding projects and improved healthcare and education.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski201123St._John2012149-86] House building became a major social priority to eliminate homelessness and replace the [shanty towns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shanty_town) created by Libya's growing urbanization.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198673-82] The health sector was also expanded; by 1978, Libya had 50 per cent more hospitals than it had in 1968, while the number of doctors had increased from 700 to over 3,000 in that decade.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198674-87] [Malaria](20423.md) was eradicated, and [trachoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trachoma) and [tuberculosis](30653.md) greatly curtailed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198674-87] Compulsory education was expanded from 6 to 9 years, while adult literacy programs and free university education were introduced.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198638-88] [Beida University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayda,_Libya#Education) was founded, while [Tripoli University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tripoli_University) and [Benghazi University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benghazi_University) were expanded.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198638-88] In doing so, the government helped integrate the poor into the education system.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198674–75-89] Through these measures, the RCC greatly expanded the [public sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_sector), providing employment for thousands.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski201123St._John2012149-86] These early social programs proved popular within Libya,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198619Kawczynski201122St._John2012149-90] partly due to Gaddafi's personal charisma, youth, underdog status as a Bedouin, and his rhetoric emphasizing himself as a successor to the anti-Italian fighter [Omar al-Mukhtar](1136889.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle2008b31–32Kawczynski201122-91]

To combat the country's strong regional and tribal divisions, the RCC promoted the idea of a unified pan-Libyan identity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012154-92] They discredited tribal leaders as agents of the old regime, and in August 1971 a Sabha military court tried many of them for counter-revolutionary activity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012154-92] Long-standing administrative boundaries were re-drawn, crossing tribal boundaries, while pro-revolutionary modernizers replaced traditional leaders, yet the communities they served often rejected them.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012154–155-93] Realizing the failures of the modernizers, Gaddafi created the [Arab Socialist Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Socialist_Union_\(Libya\)) (ASU) in June 1971, a mass mobilization [vanguard party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vanguard_party) with him as president.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986136–137BlundyLycett198791Vandewalle200683Vandewalle2008b11St._John2012155-94] The ASU recognized the RCC as its "Supreme Leading Authority", and was designed to further revolutionary enthusiasm.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198791Vandewalle2008b11St._John2012155-95] It remained heavily bureaucratic and failed to mobilize mass support as Gaddafi had envisioned.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986138-96]

#### Foreign relations

Further information: [Foreign relations of Libya under Muammar Gaddafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Libya_under_Muammar_Gaddafi)



Gaddafi (left) with Egyptian President Nasser in 1969. Nasser privately described Gaddafi as "a nice boy, but terribly naïve".

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198718-97]

The influence of Nasser's Arab nationalism over the RCC was immediately apparent.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle200679–80Vandewalle2008b9St._John2012137-98] The administration was instantly [recognized](524626.md) by the neighbouring Arab nationalist regimes in Egypt, Syria, Iraq, and Sudan,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198655BlundyLycett198760Kawczynski201118-99] with Egypt sending experts to aid the inexperienced RCC.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198762–63Kawczynski201118-100] Gaddafi propounded [pan-Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Arab) ideas, proclaiming the need for a single Arab state stretching across North Africa and the Middle East.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198696BlundyLycett198775Kawczynski201165St._John2012186-101] In December 1969, Libya signed the Tripoli Charter alongside Egypt and Sudan. This established the Arab Revolutionary Front, a pan-national union designed as a first step towards the eventual political unification of the three nations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198664BlundyLycett198775Kawczynski201165St._John2012186-102] In 1970 Syria declared its intention to join.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198696-103]

Nasser died unexpectedly in September 1970, with Gaddafi playing a prominent role at his funeral.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198666-104] Nasser was succeeded by [Anwar Sadat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anwar_Sadat), who replaced the idea of a unified state with [a political federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation_of_Arab_Republics), implemented in April 1971; in doing so, Egypt, Syria, and Sudan received large grants of Libyan oil money.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198697Harris198687Kawczynski201165St._John2012151–152-105] In July 1971, Gaddafi sided with Sadat against the Soviet Union in the [1971 Sudanese coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1971_Sudanese_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) and dispatched Libyan fighter jets to force down a [British Overseas Airways Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Overseas_Airways_Corporation) jetliner carrying the leading coup plotters, [Farouk Osman Hamadallah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farouk_Osman_Hamadallah) and Babikir al-Nour. They were extradited back to [Khartoum](17320.md), where they were promptly executed by Sudanese leader [Jaafar Nimeiry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaafar_Nimeiry).[^cite_note-Korn_88-106] In February 1972, Gaddafi and Sadat signed an unofficial charter of merger, but it was never implemented because relations broke down the next year. Sadat became increasingly wary of Libya's radical direction, and the September 1973 deadline for implementing the Federation passed with no action taken.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski201166St._John2012182-107]

After the 1969 coup, representatives of the [Four Powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allied_Control_Council)—France, the UK, the US, and the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union)—were called to meet RCC representatives.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012140-108] The UK and the US quickly extended diplomatic recognition, hoping to secure their military bases and fearing further instability. Hoping to ingratiate themselves with Gaddafi, in 1970 the US informed him of at least one planned counter-coup.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198765Kawczynski201118St._John2012140–141-109] Such attempts to form a working relationship with the RCC failed; Gaddafi was determined to reassert national sovereignty and expunge what he described as foreign colonial and imperialist influences. His administration insisted that the US and the UK remove their military bases from Libya. The British left in March and the Americans in June 1970.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198676–77BlundyLycett198761Kawczynski201119St._John2012141–143-110]

Moving to reduce Italian influence, in October 1970 all Italian-owned assets were expropriated, and the 12,000-strong [Italian community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Libyans) was expelled alongside the smaller community of [Libyan Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Libya). The day became a [national holiday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_holiday) known as "Vengeance Day".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198672BlundyLycett198764Kawczynski201121–22St._John2012142-111] Italy complained that this was in contravention of the 1956 Italo-Libyan Treaty, although no UN sanctions came.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198672-112] Aiming to reduce [NATO](21133.md) power in the Mediterranean, in 1971 Libya requested that [Malta](19137.md) cease allowing NATO to use its land for a military base, in turn offering Malta foreign aid. Compromising, Malta's government continued allowing NATO to use the island, but only on the condition that NATO would not use it for attacks on Arab territory.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John1983475St._John198787–88St._John2012150–151-113] Over the coming decade, Libya developed stronger political and economic links with [Dom Mintoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dom_Mintoff)'s Maltese administration, and under Libya's urging Malta did not renew the UK's airbases in 1980.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986117-114] Orchestrating a military build-up, the RCC began purchasing weapons from France and the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John198774–75St._John2012144–145-115] The commercial relationship with the latter led to an increasingly strained relationship with the US, which was then engaged in the [Cold War](325329.md) with the Soviets.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012144–145-116]

A 1973 anti-Gaddafist British [newsreel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newsreel) including an interview with Gaddafi about his support for foreign militants

Gaddafi was especially critical of the US due to its support of Israel and sided with the [Palestinians](23267.md) in the [Israeli–Palestinian conflict](46216.md), viewing the creation of Israel as a Western colonial occupation forced upon the [Arab world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_world).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198770–71Vandewalle2008b34Kawczynski201164St._John2012150–152-117] He believed Palestinian violence against Israeli and Western targets the justified response of an oppressed people fighting against colonization.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986114-118] Calling on Arab states to wage "continuous war" against Israel, in 1970 he initiated a Jihad Fund to finance anti-Israeli militants.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John1983478BlundyLycett198771St._John198736St._John2012185-119] In June 1972 Gaddafi created the First Nasserite Volunteers Centre to train anti-Israeli guerrillas.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski201137St._John2012151-120]

Like Nasser, Gaddafi favoured the Palestinian leader [Yasser Arafat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasser_Arafat) and his group, [Fatah](11104.md), over more militant and [Marxist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxism) Palestinian groups.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198664–65St._John198737-121] As the years progressed however, Gaddafi's relationship with Arafat became strained, with Gaddafi considering him too moderate and calling for more violent action.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986116BlundyLycett198769–70Kawczynski201137St._John2012178-122] Instead, he supported militias like the [Popular Front for the Liberation of Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_Front_for_the_Liberation_of_Palestine), [Popular Front for the Liberation of Palestine – General Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_Front_for_the_Liberation_of_Palestine_%E2%80%93_General_Command), the [Democratic Front for the Liberation of Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Front_for_the_Liberation_of_Palestine), [As-Sa'iqa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/As-Sa'iqa), the [Palestinian Popular Struggle Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Popular_Struggle_Front), and the [Abu Nidal Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Nidal_Organization).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986116BlundyLycett1987150St._John200895-123] He funded the [Black September Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_September_Organization) which perpetrated the 1972 [Munich massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich_massacre) of Israeli athletes in West Germany and flew the militants' bodies to Libya for a hero's funeral.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986114BlundyLycett198778Kawczynski201138St._John2012178-124]

Gaddafi financially supported other militant groups across the world, including the [Black Panther Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Panther_Party), the [Nation of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation_of_Islam), the [Almighty Black P. Stone Nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almighty_Black_P._Stone_Nation), the [Tupamaros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tupamaros), the [19th of April Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/19th_of_April_Movement) and the [Sandinista National Liberation Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandinista_National_Liberation_Front) in Nicaragua, the [ANC](2503.md) in South Africa, the [Provisional Irish Republican Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provisional_Irish_Republican_Army), [ETA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ETA_\(separatist_group\)), [Action Directe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Action_Directe), the [Red Brigades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Brigades), and the [Red Army Faction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Army_Faction) in Europe, and the [Armenian Secret Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_Secret_Army_for_the_Liberation_of_Armenia), the [Japanese Red Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_Red_Army), the [Free Aceh Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Aceh_Movement), and the [Moro National Liberation Front](2821886.md) in the [Philippines](23440.md).[^cite_note-125] Gaddafi was indiscriminate in the causes he funded, sometimes switching sides in a conflict, as in the [Eritrean War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eritrean_War_of_Independence).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198778–81,_150,_185Kawczynski201134–35,_40–53St._John2012151-126] Throughout the 1970s these groups received financial support from Libya, which became a leader in the [Third World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_World)'s struggle against [colonialism](7299.md) and [neocolonialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neocolonialism).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198778–81,_150Kawczynski201134–35,_40–53St._John2012151-127] Though many of these groups were labelled "[terrorists](30636.md)" by critics, Gaddafi rejected this characterization, instead considering them revolutionaries engaged in liberation struggles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198655-128]

### The "Popular Revolution": 1973–1977



Gaddafi with Romanian President [Nicolae Ceaușescu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolae_Ceau%C8%99escu) in Bucharest, 1974

On 16 April 1973, Gaddafi proclaimed the start of a "Popular Revolution" in a speech at [Zuwarah](2010687.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986139BlundyLycett198785Vandewalle200682Vandewalle2008b12Kawczynski201122St._John2012156-129] He initiated this with a five-point plan, the first point of which dissolved all existing laws, to be replaced by revolutionary enactments. The second point proclaimed that all opponents of the revolution had to be removed, while the third initiated an administrative revolution that Gaddafi proclaimed would remove all traces of [bureaucracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureaucracy) and the [bourgeoisie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bourgeoisie). The fourth point announced that the population must form People's Committees and be armed to defend the revolution, while the fifth proclaimed the beginning of the [Cultural Revolution in Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_Revolution_in_Libya), to expunge the country of "poisonous" foreign influences.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986140Harris198618BlundyLycett198785–86Kawczynski201122St._John2012156-130] He began to lecture on this new phase of the revolution in Libya, Egypt, and France.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198793–94-131] As a process, it had many similarities with the [Cultural Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_Revolution) implemented in China.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986140-132]

As part of this Popular Revolution, Gaddafi invited Libya's people to found [General People's Committees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_People's_Committee) as conduits for raising political consciousness. Although offering little guidance for how to set up these councils, Gaddafi claimed that they would offer a form of [direct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direct_democracy) [political participation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Participatory_democracy) that was more democratic than a traditional party-based [representative system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Representative_democracy). He hoped that the councils would mobilize the people behind the RCC, erode the power of the traditional leaders and the bureaucracy, and allow for a new legal system chosen by the people.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEl-Khawas198420BlundyLycett198786St._John2012156-133] Many such committees were established in schools and colleges,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEl-Khawas198420Bearman1986140-134] where they were responsible for vetting staff, courses, and textbooks to determine if they were compatible with the country's revolutionary ideology.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986140-132]

The People's Committees led to a high percentage of public involvement in decision making, within the limits permitted by the RCC,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012157-135] but exacerbated tribal divisions and tensions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavis198278BlundyLycett1987103–104-136] They also served as a surveillance system, aiding the security services in locating individuals with views critical of the RCC, leading to the arrest of [Ba'athists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ba'athism), [Marxists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxism), and [Islamists](15012.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986141Harris198618BlundyLycett1987116St._John2012157-137] Operating in a pyramid structure, the base form of these Committees were local working groups, who sent elected representatives to the district level, and from there to the national level, divided between the [General People's Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_People's_Congress_\(Libya\)) and the [General People's Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_People's_Committee).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett1987104Kawczynski201126-138] Above these remained Gaddafi and the RCC, who remained responsible for all major decisions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198664St._John2012163-139] In crossing regional and tribal identities, the committee system aided national integration and centralization and tightened Gaddafi's control over the state and administrative apparatus.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986141-140]

#### Third International Theory and *The Green Book*

Main articles: [Third International Theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_International_Theory) and [The Green Book (Muammar Gaddafi)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Green_Book_\(Muammar_Gaddafi\))

In June 1973, Gaddafi created a political ideology as a basis for the Popular Revolution: [Third International Theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_International_Theory). This approach regarded both the US and the Soviet Union as imperialist and thus rejected Western [capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitalism) as well as Marxist–Leninist atheism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986150BlundyLycett198786–87St._John2012157–158-141] In this respect, it was similar to the [Three Worlds Theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_Worlds_Theory) developed by China's political leader [Mao Zedong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mao_Zedong).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198658-142] As part of this theory, Gaddafi praised [nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalism) as a progressive force and advocated the creation of a pan-Arab state which would lead the Islamic and Third Worlds against imperialism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012158-143] Gaddafi saw Islam as having a key role in this ideology, calling for an [Islamic revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_revival) that returned to the origins of the [Qur'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qur'an), rejecting scholarly interpretations and the [Hadith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith); in doing so, he angered many Libyan clerics.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198649BlundyLycett1987122St._John2012159-144] During 1973 and 1974, his government deepened the legal reliance on *sharia*, for instance by introducing [flogging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flagellation) as punishment for those convicted of adultery or homosexual activity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986163BlundyLycett1987112-145]

Gaddafi summarized Third International Theory in three short volumes published between 1975 and 1979, collectively known as *[The Green Book](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Green_Book_\(Muammar_Gaddafi\))*. Volume one was devoted to the issue of democracy, outlining the flaws of representative systems in favour of direct, participatory GPCs. The second dealt with Gaddafi's beliefs regarding socialism, while the third explored social issues regarding the family and the tribe. While the first two volumes advocated radical reform, the third adopted a [socially conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_conservatism) stance, proclaiming that while men and women were equal, they were biologically designed for different roles in life.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198796–100Vandewalle2008b19Kawczynski201124St._John2012161–165-146] During the years that followed, Gaddafists adopted quotes from *The Green Book*, such as "Representation is Fraud", as slogans.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012162-147] Meanwhile, in September 1975, Gaddafi implemented further measures to increase popular mobilization, introducing objectives to improve the relationship between the Councils and the ASU.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012165-148]

In 1975, Gaddafi's government declared a [state monopoly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_monopoly) on foreign trade.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986145–146-149] Its increasingly radical reforms, coupled with the large amount of oil revenue being spent on foreign causes, generated discontent in Libya,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle2008b18Kawczynski201123-150] particularly among the country's merchant class.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986146-151] In 1974, Libya saw its first civilian attack on Gaddafi's government when a Benghazi army building was bombed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett1987114-152] Much of the opposition centred around RCC member [Umar Muhayshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omar_Mehishi). With fellow RCC members [Bashir Saghir al-Hawaadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bashir_Saghir_Hawadi) and Awad Ali Hamza, he began plotting a coup against Gaddafi.[^cite_note-153] In 1975, their plot was exposed and Muhayshi fled to Tunisia, eventually receiving asylum from Sadat's Egypt.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986146BlundyLycett1987118Vandewalle2008b18Kawczynski201123St._John2012165-154] Hawaadi, Hamza, and [Omar El-Hariri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omar_El-Hariri) were arrested. Most of the other conspirators were executed in March 1976.[^cite_note-\:0-155] Another RCC member, foreign minister [Abdul-Munim al-Huni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdel_Moneim_al-Houni), also fled to Egypt.[^cite_note-156][^cite_note-157] In the aftermath, only five RCC members remained: Gaddafi, Jalloud, [Abu-Bakr Yunis Jabr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu-Bakr_Yunis_Jabr), [Mustafa Kharubi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustafa_Kharubi), and [Kweldi al-Hamidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khweldi_Hameidi).[^cite_note-158][^cite_note-159][^cite_note-160] Thus, power was further concentrated in Gaddafi's hands.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986147-161] This ultimately led to the RCC's official abolition in March 1977.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012165-148]

In September 1975, Gaddafi purged the army, arresting around 200 senior officers, and in October he founded the clandestine Office for the Security of the Revolution.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett1987118–119-162] In April 1976, he called upon his supporters in universities to establish "revolutionary student councils" and drive out "reactionary elements".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986148-163] During that year, anti-Gaddafist student demonstrations broke out at the universities of Tripoli and Benghazi, resulting in clashes with both Gaddafist students and police. The RCC responded with mass arrests and introduced compulsory [national service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_service) for young people.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986148BlundyLycett1987119–120Vandewalle2008b18Kawczynski201123-164] In January 1977, two dissenting students and a number of army officers were publicly hanged; [Amnesty International](18947898.md) condemned it as the first time in Gaddafist Libya that dissenters had been executed for purely political crimes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett1987121–122-165] Dissent also arose from conservative clerics and the Muslim Brotherhood, who accused Gaddafi of moving towards Marxism and criticized his abolition of private property as being against the Islamic *[sunnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunnah)*; these forces were then persecuted as anti-revolutionary,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986162BlundyLycett1987122–123Kawczynski201129–30-166] while all privately owned Islamic colleges and universities were shut down.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986148-163]

#### Foreign relations

After Anwar Sadat's ascension to the Egyptian presidency, Libya's relations with Egypt deteriorated.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198688BlundyLycett198774,_93–94Kawczynski201166-167] Over the coming years, the two slipped into a state of [cold war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_war_\(general_term\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986166–167-168] Sadat was perturbed by Gaddafi's unpredictability and insistence that Egypt required a cultural revolution akin to that being carried out in Libya.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198688BlundyLycett198774,_93–94Kawczynski201166-167] In February 1973, Israeli forces shot down [Libyan Arab Airlines Flight 114](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libyan_Arab_Airlines_Flight_114), which had strayed from Egyptian airspace into Israeli-held territory during a sandstorm. Gaddafi's foreign minister [Salah Busir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salah_Busir) was on board and allegedly targeted by Israel in retaliation for the [Munich massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich_massacre).[^cite_note-169] Gaddafi was infuriated that Egypt had not done more to prevent the incident, and in retaliation planned to destroy the *[Queen Elizabeth 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Elizabeth_2)*, a British ship chartered by American Jews to sail to [Haifa](55606.md) for Israel's 25th anniversary. Gaddafi ordered an Egyptian submarine to target the ship, but Sadat cancelled the order, fearing a military escalation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986114–115Harris198687BlundyLycett198782–83St._John198755Kawczynski201166–67-170]



Gaddafi in 1976 with a child on his lap

Gaddafi was later infuriated when Egypt and Syria planned the [Yom Kippur War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yom_Kippur_War) against Israel without consulting him and was angered when Egypt conceded to peace talks rather than continuing the war.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman198699–100Harris198687Kawczynski201167St._John2012182–183-171] Gaddafi became openly hostile to Egypt's leader, calling for Sadat's overthrow.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski201167-172] When Sudanese President [Gaafar Nimeiry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaafar_Nimeiry) took Sadat's side, Gaddafi also spoke out against him, encouraging the [Sudan People's Liberation Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudan_People's_Liberation_Army)'s attempt to overthrow Nimeiry.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986167BlundyLycett1987185St._John198762Kawczynski201179–80St._John2012191-173] In 1974, Gaddafi released [Abdul-Aziz Shennib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul-Aziz_Shennib), a commander under [King Idris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idris_of_Libya), from prison and appointed him Libyan ambassador to [Jordan](7515964.md). Shennib had attended the [Royal Military Academy Sandhurst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Military_Academy_Sandhurst) with [King Hussein of Jordan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hussein_of_Jordan) and was tasked by Gaddafi with Hussein's assassination. Shennib instead informed Hussein of the plot and defected to Jordan.[^cite_note-\:7-33] Relations with Syria also soured over the events in the [Lebanese Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanese_Civil_War). Initially, both Libya and Syria had contributed troops to the Arab League's peacekeeping force, although after the Syrian army attacked the [Lebanese National Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanese_National_Movement), Gaddafi openly accused Syrian President [Hafez al-Assad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafez_al-Assad) of "national treason"; he was the only Arab leader to criticize Syria's actions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986165–166-174] In late 1972 and early 1973, Libya invaded Chad to annex the uranium-rich [Aouzou Strip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aouzou_Strip).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett1987181St._John2012187-175]

Intent on propagating Islam, in 1973 Gaddafi founded the Islamic Call Society, which had opened 132 centres across Africa within a decade.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986106–107Harris1986103–104BlundyLycett198793,_122St._John2012186-176] In 1973 he converted Gabonese President [Omar Bongo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omar_Bongo), an action which he repeated three years later with [Jean-Bédel Bokassa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-B%C3%A9del_Bokassa), president of the [Central African Republic](5478.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski201177–78-177] Between 1973 and 1979, Libya provided \$500 million in aid to African countries, namely to Zaire and Uganda, and founded joint-venture companies throughout the countries to aid trade and development.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John198796-178] Gaddafi was also keen on reducing Israeli influence within Africa, using financial incentives to successfully convince eight African states to break off diplomatic relations with Israel in 1973.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986107–109Harris198688St._John198794Kawczynski201177St._John2012184-179]

A strong relationship was also established between Gaddafi's Libya and Prime Minister [Zulfikar Ali Bhutto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zulfikar_Ali_Bhutto)'s Pakistani government, with the two countries exchanging nuclear research and military assistance. In recognition of Gaddafi's support of [Pakistan's right to pursue nuclear weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N-deterrence) and financial support for the "Islamic bomb", Lahore Stadium was renamed [Gaddafi Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaddafi_Stadium).[^cite_note-180][^cite_note-181][^cite_note-\:2-182] Gaddafi also provided support for Pakistan in the [Bangladesh Liberation War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Liberation_War); he reportedly deployed [F-5s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northrop_F-5) to [Sargodha AFB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sargodha_Airbase) and penned a strongly worded letter to [Indian Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Prime_Minister) [Indira Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Gandhi) accusing her of aggression against Pakistan.[^cite_note-Pen_and_Sword,_Bowman-183][^cite_note-THe_News_International-184] Gaddafi's strong relationship with Pakistan ended after Bhutto was [deposed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Fair_Play) by [Muhammad Zia-ul-Haq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Zia-ul-Haq) in 1977 as Zia distrusted Gaddafi and rejected further Libyan financing for the [Pakistani nuclear program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_nuclear_programme) in favor of Saudi financing.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986169-185][^cite_note-\:2-182][^cite_note-186]

Gaddafi sought to develop closer links in the [Maghreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghreb); in January 1974 Libya and Tunisia announced a political union, the [Arab Islamic Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Islamic_Republic). Although advocated by Gaddafi and Tunisian President [Habib Bourguiba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habib_Bourguiba), the move was deeply unpopular in Tunisia, and it was soon abandoned.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986100–101BlundyLycett198776Kawczynski201171–72St._John2012183-187] Retaliating, Gaddafi sponsored anti-government militants in Tunisia into the 1980s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski201172St._John2012183-188] Turning his attention to [Algeria](358.md), in 1975 Libya signed, in [Hassi Messaoud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hassi_Messaoud), a defensive alliance allegedly to counter alleged "Moroccan expansionism", also funding the [Polisario Front](201574.md) of [Western Sahara](33220.md) in its independence struggle against [Morocco](19291.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986170Kawczynski201171St._John2012183-189] Seeking to diversify Libya's economy, Gaddafi's government began purchasing shares in major European corporations like [Fiat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiat) as well as buying real estate in Malta and Italy, which would become a valuable source of income during the [1980s oil slump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980s_oil_glut).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris1986114BlundyLycett1987199–201-190]

## Great Socialist People's Libyan Arab Jamahiriya

### Foundation: 1977

On 2 March 1977, the General People's Congress adopted the "[Declaration on the Establishment of the Authority of the People](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_on_the_Establishment_of_the_Authority_of_the_People)" at Gaddafi's behest. Dissolving the Libyan Arab Republic, it was replaced by the Great Socialist People's Libyan Arab Jamahiriya ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): الجماهيرية العربية الليبية الشعبية الاشتراكية, **al-Jamāhīrīyah al-‘Arabīyah al-Lībīyah ash-Sha‘bīyah al-Ishtirākīyah*)*, a "state of the masses" conceptualized by Gaddafi.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986154–155BlundyLycett1987105Kawczynski201126–27St._John2012166–168-191] A new, all-green banner was adopted as the country's flag.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986155-192] Officially, the *Jamahiriya* was a [direct democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direct_democracy) in which the people ruled themselves through the 187 [Basic People's Congresses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basic_People's_Congress_\(political\)) (BPCs), where all adult Libyans participated and voted on national decisions. These then sent members to the annual General People's Congress, which was broadcast live on television. In principle, the People's Congresses were Libya's highest authority, with major decisions proposed by government officials or with Gaddafi himself requiring the consent of the People's Congresses.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEl-Khawas198427BlundyLycett198729St._John2012166–168Vandewalle2008b19–20-193] Gaddafi became General Secretary of the GPC, although he stepped down from this position in early 1979 and appointed himself "Leader of the Revolution".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John198713-194]



[Flag of Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Libya) (1977–2011)

Although all political control was officially vested in the People's Congresses, in reality Libya's existing political leadership continued to exercise varying degrees of power and influence.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986155-192] Debate remained limited, and major decisions regarding the economy and defence were avoided or dealt with cursorily; the GPC largely remained "a rubber stamp" for Gaddafi's policies.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198729-195] On rare occasions, the GPC opposed Gaddafi's suggestions, sometimes successfully; notably, when Gaddafi called on primary schools to be abolished, believing that [homeschooling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Home_schooling) was healthier for children, the GPC rejected the idea.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198729-195] In other instances, Gaddafi pushed through laws without the GPC's support, such as when he desired to allow women into the armed forces.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198667–68-196] At other times, he ordered snap elections when it appeared that the GPC would enact laws he opposed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John1987133–134-197] Gaddafi proclaimed that the People's Congresses provided for Libya's every political need, rendering other political organizations unnecessary; all non-authorized groups, including political parties, professional associations, independent trade unions, and women's groups, were banned.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski201127St._John2012166–168-198] Despite these restrictions, [Ronald Bruce St. John](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ronald_Bruce_St._John) noted that the *Jamahiriya* system still "introduced a level of representation and participation hitherto unknown in Libya".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John1987134-199]

With preceding legal institutions abolished, Gaddafi envisioned the *Jamahiriya* as following the [Qur'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qur'an) for legal guidance, adopting *sharia* law; he proclaimed "man-made" laws unnatural and dictatorial, only permitting [Allah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Islam)'s law.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski201127–28St._John2012167-200] Within a year he was backtracking, announcing that *sharia* was inappropriate for the *Jamahiriya* because it guaranteed the protection of private property, contravening *The Green Book*'s socialism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle2008b28-201] His emphasis on placing his own work on a par with the Qur'an led conservative clerics to accuse him of *[shirk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shirk_\(Islam\))*, furthering their opposition to his regime.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198650-202] In July 1977, a [border war broke out](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian%E2%80%93Libyan_War) with Egypt, in which the Egyptians defeated Libya despite their technological inferiority. The conflict lasted one week before both sides agreed to sign a peace treaty that was brokered by several Arab states.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986170–171BlundyLycett1987105Vandewalle2008b35Kawczynski201167–68St._John2012183-203] Both Egypt and Sudan had aligned themselves with the US, and this pushed Libya into a strategic, although not political, alignment with the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986168-204] In recognition of the growing commercial relationship between Libya and the Soviets, Gaddafi was invited to visit Moscow in December 1976; there, he entered talks with [Leonid Brezhnev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonid_Brezhnev).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986169St._John198776St._John2012180-205] In August 1977, he visited [Yugoslavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Federal_Republic_of_Yugoslavia), where he met its leader [Josip Broz Tito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josip_Broz_Tito), with whom he had a much warmer relationship.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986169-185] He also enjoyed a warm relationship with [Romanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Republic_of_Romania) leader [Nicolae Ceaușescu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolae_Ceau%C8%99escu). According to Romanian spy chief [Ion Mihai Pacepa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ion_Mihai_Pacepa), Gaddafi once exclaimed to Ceaușescu, "My brother! You are my brother for the rest of my life!"[^cite_note-206] After Pacepa defected to the US in July 1978, Gaddafi and [Yasser Arafat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasser_Arafat) contributed \$1 million each to Ceaușescu's \$4 million bounty on Pacepa.[^cite_note-207]

### Revolutionary Committees and furthering socialism: 1978–1980

> If socialism is defined as a redistribution of wealth and resources, a socialist revolution clearly occurred in Libya after 1969 and most especially in the second half of the 1970s. The management of the economy was increasingly socialist in intent and effect with wealth in housing, capital and land significantly redistributed or in the process of redistribution. Private enterprise was virtually eliminated, largely replaced by a centrally controlled economy.

— Libyan Studies scholar [Ronald Bruce St. John](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ronald_Bruce_St._John)

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012173-208]

In December 1978, Gaddafi stepped down as Secretary-General of the GPC, announcing his new focus on revolutionary rather than government activities; this was part of his new emphasis on separating the apparatus of the revolution from government. Although no longer in a formal government post, he adopted the title of "Leader of the Revolution" and continued as commander-in-chief of the armed forces.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle2008b26Kawczynski20113St._John2012169-209] Historian Dirk Vandewalle stated that despite the Jamahariya's claims to being a direct democracy, Libya remained "an exclusionary political system whose decision-making process" was "restricted to a small cadre of advisers and confidantes" surrounding Gaddafi.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle20066-210]

Libya started constructing a welfare state. In March 1978, the government issued guidelines for housing redistribution, attempting to ensure every adult owned their own home. Most families were banned from owning more than one house, while former rental properties were expropriated by the state and sold to the tenants at a heavily subsidized price.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John1983484BlundyLycett1987111Kawczynski2011221St._John2012171–172-211] In September, Gaddafi called for the People's Committees to eliminate the "bureaucracy of the public sector" and the "dictatorship of the private sector"; the People's Committees took control of several hundred companies, converting them into [worker cooperatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Worker_cooperative) run by elected representatives.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986191BlundyLycett1987110–111St._John2012168-212]

In March 1979, the GPC announced the separation of government and revolution, the latter being represented by new Revolutionary Committees, who operated with the People's Committees in schools, universities, unions, the police force, and the military.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett1987116–117,_127Vandewalle2008b25–26Kawczynski201131St._John2012169–171-213] Dominated by revolutionary zealots, mostly youths, the Revolutionary Committees were based in Tripoli and met with Gaddafi annually.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986187–189BlundyLycett1987116–117,_127Vandewalle2008b25–26Kawczynski201131St._John2012169–171-214] Membership was drawn from within the BPCs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John1987134-199] The revolutionary committee system became "a key—if not the main—mechanism through which \[Gaddafi] exercises political control in Libya".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986189-215] Publishing a weekly magazine, *The Green March*, starting October 1980 they took control of the press.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett1987116–117,_127Vandewalle2008b25–26Kawczynski201131St._John2012169–171-213] Responsible for perpetuating the revolution, they performed ideological surveillance, adopting a significant security role, making arrests and putting people on trial according to the "law of the revolution".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986189BlundyLycett1987116–117,_127Vandewalle2008b25–26Kawczynski201131St._John2012169–171-216] With no legal or safeguards, the administration of revolutionary justice was largely arbitrary and resulted in widespread abuse and the suppression of [civil liberties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_liberties): the "Green Terror".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett1987117Vandewalle2008b28St._John2012174-217]

In 1979, the committees began the redistribution of land in the [Jefara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jafara) plain, continuing through 1981.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986275St._John2012172-218] In May 1980, measures to redistribute and equalize wealth were implemented; anyone with over 1000 [dinar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libyan_dinar) in their bank account saw that extra money expropriated.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John1983484BlundyLycett1987128Kawczynski2011221St._John2012172-219] The next year, the GPC announced that the government would take control of all import, export and distribution functions, with state supermarkets replacing privately owned businesses; this led to a decline in the availability of consumer goods and the development of a thriving [black market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_market).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986195BlundyLycett198728Vandewalle2008b21Kawczynski2011220St._John2012172-220] Gaddafi was frustrated by the slow pace of social reform on women's issues, and in 1979 launched a Revolutionary Women's Formation, to replace the more gradualist Libyan General Women's Federation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986199-221] In 1978 he had established a Women's Military Academy in Tripoli, encouraging all women to enlist for training.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986241-222] The measure was hugely controversial and voted down by the GPC in February 1983. Gaddafi remained adamant, and when it was again voted down by the GPC in March 1984, he refused to abide by the decision, declaring that "he who opposes the training and emancipation of women is an agent of imperialism, whether he likes it or not."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986241–243-223]

The Jamahiriya's radical direction earned the government many enemies. Most internal opposition came from [Islamic fundamentalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_fundamentalism), inspired by the events of the 1979 [Iranian Revolution](347268.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986246-224] In February 1978, Gaddafi discovered that his head of military intelligence was plotting to kill him and increasingly entrusted security to his Qadhadfa tribe.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett1987127–128Vandewalle2008b19-225] Many who had seen their wealth confiscated turned against the administration, and Western-funded opposition groups were founded by exiles. Most prominent was the [National Front for the Salvation of Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Front_for_the_Salvation_of_Libya) (NFSL), which orchestrated militant attacks against Libya's government.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986247–248Harris198679Vandewalle2008b32St._John2012173–174-226] Another, al-Borkan, began killing Libyan diplomats abroad.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986248–249Harris198679BlundyLycett1987156-227] Following Gaddafi's command to kill these "stray dogs", the Revolutionary Committees set up overseas branches to suppress counter-revolutionary activity, assassinating dissidents.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986246BlundyLycett1987133–137Vandewalle2008b27St._John2012171-228] Although Syria and Israel also employed hit squads, Gaddafi was unusual in publicly bragging about his use of them;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett1987138-229] in April 1980, he ordered all dissidents to return home by 10 June or be "liquidated wherever you are".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986246BlundyLycett1987138-230][^cite_note-\:5-231] Within a three months period in 1980, at least ten Libyan dissidents were murdered in Europe, including ex-diplomats, ex-army officers, businessmen, journalists, and student activists in disparate locations such as [London](17867.md), [Greece](12108.md) and [Austria](26964606.md). At least eleven more were assassinated in 1981.[^cite_note-232] In 1984, Gaddafi was tricked by Egyptian President [Hosni Mubarak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hosni_Mubarak) into announcing the assassination of former Libyan Prime Minister [Abdul Hamid al-Bakkoush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Hamid_al-Bakkoush) in Cairo; Bakkhoush not only turned up alive but held a press conference with Egypt's Interior Minister.[^cite_note-233][^cite_note-234] In 1979, Gaddafi created the [Islamic Legion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Legion), through which several thousand Africans were military trained.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John200895-235]

Libya had sought to improve US relations under President [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter), for instance by courting his brother, businessman [Billy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billy_Carter), and paying for the services of former CIA officers,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986227–228-236][^cite_note-237] but in 1979 the US placed Libya on its list of "[State Sponsors of Terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Sponsors_of_Terrorism)".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012179-238] Relations were further damaged when a [demonstration torched the US embassy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_U.S._embassy_burning_in_Libya) in solidarity with the perpetrators of the [Iran hostage crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_hostage_crisis).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986228-239] Libyan fighters began intercepting US fighter jets flying over the Mediterranean, signalling the collapse of relations between the countries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012179-238] Italian media have alleged that the [Itavia Flight 870](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Itavia_Flight_870) was shot down during a [dogfight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dogfight) involving [Libyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libyan_Air_Force), [United States](32090.md), [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Air_Force) and [Italian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Air_Force) fighters in an assassination attempt by [NATO](21133.md) members on a Libyan politician, perhaps even Gaddafi, flying in the same airspace.[^cite_note-240][^cite_note-241]

Libyan relations with Lebanon and [Shi'ite](26961.md) communities deteriorated due to the 1978 disappearance of Imam [Musa al-Sadr](2432466.md) when visiting Libya; the Lebanese accused Gaddafi of having him killed or imprisoned, a charge he denied.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett1987157–158St._John198741Kawczynski201170–71St._John2012239-242] Relations with Pakistan broke down in this period. Despite Gaddafi's repeated appeals to [Muhammad Zia-ul-Haq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Zia-ul-Haq) to spare [Zulfikar Ali Bhutto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zulfikar_Ali_Bhutto)'s life, Bhutto was executed in 1979.[^cite_note-Congress_Publications-243] In retaliation and for Zia's refusal to share Pakistan's nuclear technology, Gaddafi began training [Al-Zulfikar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Zulfikar), an anti-Zia insurgency led by Bhutto's sons [Murtaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murtaza_Bhutto) and [Shahnawaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahnawaz_Bhutto), expelled all 150,000 Pakistanis living in Libya, and provided asylum for the [Bhutto family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhutto_family).[^cite_note-THe_News_International-184][^cite_note-Peoples'_Relation-244][^cite_note-Congress_Publications-243] Relations with Syria improved, as Gaddafi and Syrian President [Hafez al-Assad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafez_al-Assad) shared an enmity with Israel and Egypt's Sadat. In 1980, they proposed a political union, with Libya promising to pay off Syria's £1-billion debt to the Soviet Union; although pressures led Assad to pull out, they remained allies.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John198761–62Kawczynski201168–69-245] Another key ally was Uganda, and in 1979, during the [Uganda–Tanzania War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uganda%E2%80%93Tanzania_War), Gaddafi sent 2,500 troops to [defend](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_support_of_Uganda_in_the_Uganda%E2%80%93Tanzania_War#Libya) President [Idi Amin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idi_Amin) from Tanzanian invaders. The mission failed; 400 Libyans were killed, and Libya was forced to retreat.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986112BlundyLycett1987185–186Kawczynski201178–79St._John2012189-246] Gaddafi came to regret his alliance with Amin, openly criticizing him as a "[fascist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascism)" and a "show-off".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986112–13Harris1986105-247]

### Conflict with the US and its allies: 1981–1986

The early 80s saw economic trouble in Libya; from 1982 to 1986, annual oil revenues dropped from \$21 to \$5.4 billion.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198731Vandewalle2008b23Kawczynski2011104St._John2012192-248] Focusing on irrigation projects, 1983 saw construction start on Libya's largest and most expensive infrastructure project, the [Great Man-Made River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Man-Made_River); although designed to be finished by the end of the decade, it remained incomplete at the start of the 21st century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986274Harris1986119Kawczynski2011224St._John2012249-249] Military spending increased, while other administrative budgets were cut.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris1986116Vandewalle2008b35-250] Foreign debt rose,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John1987121-251] and austerity measures were introduced to promote self-reliance; in 1985 there was a mass deportation of foreign workers, mostly Egyptian and Tunisian.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John1987122-252] Domestic threats continued to plague Gaddafi; in May 1984, his [Bab al-Azizia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bab_al-Azizia) home was unsuccessfully attacked by a militia—linked to the NFSL or Muslim Brotherhood—and in the aftermath 5,000 dissidents were arrested.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986250Harris198670BlundyLycett1987178-253] In spring 1985, members of the military tried to assassinate Gaddafi twice. The first was a plot by conservative officers to assassinate him at a villa on the outskirts of Tripoli; the second was an assault on his convoy.[^cite_note-254] In November 1985, Colonel Hassan Ishkal, the third most powerful man in Libya, head of the military region of Sirte, died in a suspicious car accident. Ishkal's death was attributed to [Jalloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdessalam_Jalloud), Khalifa Hunaysh or Gaddafi.[^cite_note-255][^cite_note-256][^cite_note-257]



Construction for the Great Man-Made River Project

Libya had long supported the [FROLINAT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FROLINAT) militia in neighbouring Chad, but FROLINAT became divided over its ties to Libya in 1976.
