In January 1978, the anti-Libya faction within FROLINAT, led by [Hissène Habré](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiss%C3%A8ne_Habr%C3%A9), switched sides and allied with Chadian President [Félix Malloum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F%C3%A9lix_Malloum).[^cite_note-258][^cite_note-BuijtenhuijsA19-259] Meanwhile, the pro-Libya faction within FROLINAT, led by [Goukouni Oueddei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goukouni_Oueddei), renamed itself [People's Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Armed_Forces) (FAP). In December 1980, Gaddafi reinvaded Chad at the request of the FAP-controlled [GUNT government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transitional_Government_of_National_Unity_\(Chad\)) to aid in the civil war; in January 1981, Gaddafi suggested a political merger. The [Organisation of African Unity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_of_African_Unity) (OAU) rejected this and called for a Libyan withdrawal, which came in November 1981. The civil war resumed, and Libya sent troops back in.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986211–222BlundyLycett1987187–190Vandewalle2008b35St._John2012189–190-260]

In 1982, the GUNT government was overthrown by Habré's forces and Oueddei fled to Libya, where Gaddafi provided him with arms to continue to guerrilla war against Habré.[^cite_note-Habre-261] In November 1984, Gaddafi met with French President [François Mitterrand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Mitterrand); both agreed to withdraw from Chad.[^cite_note-262] Oueddei broke with Gaddafi in 1985 due to the former's intentions to negotiate a truce with Habré. Consequently, he was placed under house arrest by Gaddafi and allegedly arrested by Libyan police and shot in the stomach.[^cite_note-Habre-261][^cite_note-263] Oueddei survived the shooting and fled to Algeria, but continued to claim he and Gaddafi enjoyed a good relationship. When Gaddafi ordered the remnant of GUNT to attack Habré in February 1986 in violation of his agreement with Mitterrand, France launched [Operation Épervier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_%C3%89pervier), which escalated into the [Toyota War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toyota_War). Libya suffered a humiliating defeat as it was completely expelled from Chad and its commander [Khalifa Haftar](31227894.md) captured, along with 600-700 Libyan soldiers. Gaddafi disavowed Haftar and the other prisoners; one possible contributing factor to this repudiation may have been that Gaddafi had signed an agreement to withdraw Libyan forces, and Haftar's operations had been in violation of this.[^cite_note-264][^cite_note-bi-201404222-265] An embittered Haftar then joined the anti-Gaddafi [National Front for the Salvation of Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Front_for_the_Salvation_of_Libya), became a CIA asset, and was given refuge in the US.[^cite_note-Anderson-266]

Many African nations were tired of Libya's interference in their affairs; by 1980, nine African states had severed diplomatic relations,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John1987101St._John2012189-267] while in 1982 the OAU cancelled its scheduled conference in Tripoli to prevent Gaddafi gaining chairmanship.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris1986103St._John1987102Kawczynski201181St._John2012190–191-268] Some African states, however, such as [Jerry Rawlings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerry_Rawlings)'s Ghana and [Thomas Sankara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Sankara)'s Burkina Faso, had warm relations with Libya during the 1980s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaynes199062-269]

Proposing political unity with Morocco, in August 1984, Gaddafi and Moroccan monarch [Hassan II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hassan_II_of_Morocco) signed the [Oujda Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oujda_Treaty), forming the Arab–African Union; such a union was considered surprising due to the political differences and longstanding enmity that existed between the two. In a sign of warming relations, Gaddafi promised to stop funding the [Polisario Front](201574.md) and Hassan II extradited former RCC member [Umar Muhayshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umar_Muhayshi) to Libya, where he was immediately killed.[^cite_note-\:0-155] But relations deteriorated, particularly due to Morocco's friendship with the US and Israel; in August 1986, Hassan abolished the union.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986261–262BlundyLycett1987214St._John198766–67Kawczynski201172–75St._John2012216-270] Angered by the snub, Gaddafi plotted with [Abu Nidal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Nidal) to assassinate Hassan in 1987, but the plot was aborted.[^cite_note-271][^cite_note-272]

In 1981, the new US president, [Ronald Reagan](25433.md), pursued a hardline approach to Libya, viewing it as a [puppet regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puppet_regime) of the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986228–229St._John198781Kawczynski2011115–116,_120St._John2012179–180-273] Gaddafi played up his commercial relationship with the Soviets, revisiting Moscow in 1981 and 1985,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198698–99St._John198771,_78Kawczynski2011115St._John2012210–211-274] and threatening to join the [Warsaw Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Pact).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John200896-275] The Soviets were nevertheless cautious of Gaddafi, seeing him as an unpredictable extremist.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198697BlundyLycett1987183St._John198777–78-276] In August 1981, the US staged military exercises in the Gulf of Sirte – an area which Libya claimed. The US [shot down](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Sidra_incident_\(1981\)) two Libyan [Su-22](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhoi_Su-17) planes which were on an intercept course.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986230–231St._John198784Vandewalle2008b36Kawczynski2011118–119-277] Closing down Libya's embassy in Washington, Reagan advised US companies operating in Libya to reduce Americans stationed there.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle2008b37Kawczynski2011117–118St._John2012180-278] In December 1981, the [White House](33057.md) claimed Gaddafi had dispatched a hit squad to assassinate Reagan, allegedly led by [Carlos the Jackal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_the_Jackal), who had been living in Libya under Gaddafi's protection after the 1975 [OPEC siege](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OPEC_siege). Secretary of State [Alexander Haig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Haig), Defense Secretary [Caspar Weinberger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caspar_Weinberger), Counselor to the President [Edwin Meese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edwin_Meese), chief of staff [James Baker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Baker), and deputy chief of staff [Michael Deaver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Deaver) were considered potential targets and given special security. US ambassador to Italy [Maxwell M. Rabb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maxwell_M._Rabb), who was Jewish, was urgently recalled due to threats against his life. Gaddafi denied the allegations.[^cite_note-\:5-231] Gaddafi was accused of having ties to the [Lebanese Armed Revolutionary Factions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanese_Armed_Revolutionary_Factions), which had murdered US [military attaché](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_attach%C3%A9) [Charles R. Ray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_R._Ray) and Israeli diplomat [Yacov Barsimantov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killing_of_Yacov_Barsimantov) in Paris.[^cite_note-\:5-231] In March 1982, the US implemented an embargo of Libyan oil,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986231BlundyLycett1987207–208Vandewalle2008b37Kawczynski2011117–18St._John2012181-279] and in January 1986 ordered all US companies to cease operating in the country, although several hundred workers remained when the Libyan government doubled their pay.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986294BlundyLycett198727,_208Kawczynski2011117–118St._John2012176-280] In spring 1986, [the US Navy again performed exercises in the Gulf of Sirte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Action_in_the_Gulf_of_Sidra_\(1986\)); the Libyan military retaliated, but failed as the US sank Libyan ships.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986294–295Boyd-Judson200579Kawczynski2011121–122-281] Diplomatic relations also broke down with the UK, after Libyan diplomats were accused in the killing of [Yvonne Fletcher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yvonne_Fletcher), a British policewoman stationed outside their London embassy, in April 1984.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986250BlundyLycett1987175–178Vandewalle2008b37St._John2012209-282]

In 1980, Gaddafi hired former CIA agent [Edwin P. Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edwin_P._Wilson), living in Libya as a fugitive from US justice, to plot the murder of an anti-Gaddafi Libyan graduate student at [Colorado State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_State_University) named Faisal Zagallai. Zagallai was shot in the head in October 1980, in [Fort Collins, Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Collins,_Colorado) by a former [Green Beret](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Berets) and associate of Wilson named Eugene Tafoya.[^cite_note-\:6-283] Zagallai survived the attack and Tafoya was convicted of third-degree assault and conspiracy to commit assault.[^cite_note-284][^cite_note-\:6-283] Wilson was lured back to the US and sentenced to 32 years due to his ties to Gaddafi.[^cite_note-285] In 1984, Gaddafi publicly [executed Al-Sadek Hamed Al-Shuwehdy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Execution_of_Al-Sadek_Hamed_Al-Shuwehdy), an aeronautical engineer studying in the US.[^cite_note-286]

After the US accused Libya of orchestrating the [1986 Berlin discotheque bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_Berlin_discotheque_bombing), in which two US soldiers died, Reagan decided to retaliate.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986287BlundyLycett19874–5Boyd-Judson200579Kawczynski2011122-287] The CIA was critical of the move, believing Syria was a greater threat and that an attack would strengthen Gaddafi's reputation; however, Libya was recognized as a "soft target".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett19875–6-288] Reagan was supported by the UK, but opposed by other European allies, who argued it contravened international law.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris1986102Kawczynski2011123–125-289] In [Operation El Dorado Canyon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_United_States_bombing_of_Libya), orchestrated on 15 April 1986, US military planes launched air-strikes, bombing military installations, killing around 100 Libyans, including civilians. One target had been Gaddafi's home. Himself unharmed, two of Gaddafi's sons were injured, and he claimed his adopted daughter [Hanna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hana_Gaddafi) was killed, although her existence has since been questioned.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986287BlundyLycett19872–3,_7–12Boyd-Judson200579–80Vandewalle2008b37Kawczynski2011127–129-290] Gaddafi retreated to the desert to meditate.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198713,_210Kawczynski2011130-291] There were sporadic clashes between Gaddafists and army officers who wanted to overthrow the government.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198712-292] Although the US was condemned internationally, Reagan received a popularity boost at home.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoyd-Judson200580Kawczynski2011130-293] Publicly lambasting US imperialism, Gaddafi's reputation as an anti-imperialist was strengthened domestically and across the Arab world,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198715St._John2012196-294] and, in June 1986, he ordered the names of the months to be changed in Libya.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198730-295]

### "Revolution within a Revolution": 1987–1998

The late 1980s saw a series of liberalizing economic reforms within Libya designed to cope with the decline in oil revenues. In May 1987, Gaddafi announced the start of the "Revolution within a Revolution", which began with reforms to industry and agriculture and saw the re-opening of small business.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski2011225St._John2012194-296] Restrictions were placed on the activities of the Revolutionary Committees; in March 1988, their role was narrowed by the newly created Ministry for Mass Mobilization and Revolutionary Leadership to restrict their violence and judicial role, while in August 1988 Gaddafi publicly criticized them.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle2008b29St._John200897St._John2012194–195,_199–200-297] In March, hundreds of political prisoners were freed, with Gaddafi falsely claiming that there were no further political prisoners in Libya.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle2008b45St._John2012222-298] In June, Libya's government issued the Great Green Charter on Human Rights in the Era of the Masses, in which 27 articles laid out goals, rights, and guarantees to improve the situation of human rights in Libya, restricting the use of the death penalty and calling for its eventual abolition. Many of the measures suggested in the charter would be implemented the next year, although others remained inactive.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle2008b45–46St._John200897–98St._John2012197–198-299] Also in 1989, the government founded the [Al-Gaddafi International Prize for Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Gaddafi_International_Prize_for_Human_Rights), to be awarded to figures from the Third World who had struggled against colonialism and imperialism; the first year's winner was South African anti-apartheid activist [Nelson Mandela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nelson_Mandela).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012199-300] From 1994 through to 1997, the government initiated cleansing committees to root out corruption, particularly in the economic sector.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012197–198-301]

In the aftermath of the 1986 US attack, the army was purged of perceived disloyal elements,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski2011130-302] and in 1988, Gaddafi announced the creation of a popular militia to replace the army and police.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle2008b38St._John2012200-303] In 1987, [Libya began production](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libya_and_weapons_of_mass_destruction) of [mustard gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustard_gas) at a facility in Rabta, although publicly denied it was stockpiling chemical weapons,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012201–204-304] and unsuccessfully attempted to develop nuclear weapons.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski2011180–181-305] The period also saw a growth in domestic Islamist opposition, formulated into groups like the [Muslim Brotherhood](20742.md) and the [Libyan Islamic Fighting Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libyan_Islamic_Fighting_Group). Several assassination attempts against Gaddafi were foiled, and in turn, 1989 saw the security forces raid mosques believed to be centres of counter-revolutionary preaching.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski2011166–167,_236St._John2012221–222-306] In December 1993, former Libyan foreign minister [Mansour Rashid El-Kikhia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mansour_Rashid_El-Kikhia), a leader of an anti-Gaddafi coalition in exile, was abducted in Cairo.[^cite_note-307] His body was not found until 2012 in a morgue that belonged to Gaddafi's intelligence chief [Abdullah Senussi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_Senussi).[^cite_note-308]

In October 1993, elements of the increasingly marginalized army, led by officers from the powerful [Warfalla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warfalla) tribe, initiated a failed [coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1993_Libyan_coup_d'%C3%A9tat_attempt) in [Misrata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misrata) and [Bani Walid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bani_Walid) allegedly with help from the [National Front for the Salvation of Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Front_for_the_Salvation_of_Libya), [Khalifa Haftar](31227894.md), and the [CIA](5183633.md),[^cite_note-309][^cite_note-310] while in September 1995, Islamists launched an insurgency in Benghazi, and in July 1996 an anti-Gaddafist football riot broke out in Tripoli.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski2011166St._John2012223-311] In March 1996, Haftar again briefly returned to Libya to instigate an uprising against Gaddafi in the mountains of [eastern Libya](75451.md).[^cite_note-bi-201404222-265] The Revolutionary Committees experienced a resurgence to combat these Islamists.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle2008b29-312]

In 1989, Gaddafi was overjoyed by the foundation of the [Arab Maghreb Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Maghreb_Union), uniting Libya in an economic pact with Mauritania, Morocco, Tunisia, and Algeria, viewing it as beginnings of a new pan-Arab union.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski2011188St._John2012216–218-313] Gaddafi was able to recover some influence in Chad after [Hissène Habré](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiss%C3%A8ne_Habr%C3%A9) was overthrown by [Idriss Déby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idriss_D%C3%A9by) in a [Libya-sponsored coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1990_Chadian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) in 1990.[^cite_note-314][^cite_note-auto-315][^cite_note-316] Déby also gave Gaddafi detailed information about [CIA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA) operations in Chad.[^cite_note-Dictionary_of_African_Biography20122-317] Meanwhile, Libya stepped up its support for anti-Western militants such as the [Provisional IRA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provisional_Irish_Republican_Army),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012197-318] and in 1988, [Pan Am Flight 103](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan_Am_Flight_103) was blown up over [Lockerbie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockerbie) in Scotland, killing 243 passengers and 16 crew members, plus 11 people on the ground. British police investigations identified two Libyans – [Abdelbaset al-Megrahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdelbaset_al-Megrahi) and [Lamin Khalifah Fhimah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lamin_Khalifah_Fhimah) – as the chief suspects, and in November 1991 issued a declaration demanding that Libya hand them over. When Gaddafi refused, citing the [Montreal Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montreal_Convention), the United Nations (UN) imposed Resolution 748 in March 1992, initiating economic sanctions against Libya which had deep repercussions for the country's economy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoyd-Judson200580–81Vandewalle2008b39Kawczynski2011133–140St._John2012205–207-319] The country suffered an estimated US\$900 million financial loss as a result.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle2008b42-320] On 5 November 1995, US President Bill Clinton declared the US would continue to induce pressure on Libya, also recognizing that Libyan terrorists were responsible for the Lockerbie bombing.[^cite_note-321] Further problems arose with the West when in January 1989, [two Libyan warplanes were shot down by the US off the Libyan coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Sidra_incident_\(1989\)) and in September 1989, [UTA Flight 772](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UTA_Flight_772) was blown up over the Ténéré desert in Niger, killing all 170 people on board (156 passengers and 14 crew members).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012202-322]

In 1996, Gaddafi wrote a letter to the newly elected Prime Minister of Bangladesh, [Sheikh Mujibur Rahman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikh_Mujibur_Rahman)'s daughter [Sheikh Hasina](623439.md), pleading with her to spare the lives of her father's assassins [Syed Faruque Rahman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syed_Faruque_Rahman) and [Khandaker Abdur Rashid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khandaker_Abdur_Rashid). Rahman and Rashid both had business ties to Libya.[^cite_note-\:3-323][^cite_note-\:4-324]

Many Arab and African states opposed the UN sanctions, with Mandela criticizing them on a visit to Gaddafi in October 1997, when he praised Libya for its work in fighting apartheid and awarded Gaddafi the [Order of Good Hope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_Good_Hope).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoyd-Judson200573,_83Kawczynski2011147St._John2012205–206-325] They would only be suspended in 1998 when Libya agreed to allow the extradition of the suspects to the [Scottish Court in the Netherlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Court_in_the_Netherlands), in a process overseen by Mandela.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoyd-Judson200583–88Kawczynski2011146–148St._John2012206-326] As a result of the trial, Fhimah was acquitted and al-Megrahi convicted.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoyd-Judson200589-327] Privately, Gaddafi maintained that he knew nothing about who perpetrated the bombing and that Libya had nothing to do with it.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoyd-Judson200582-328]

### Pan-Africanism, reconciliation and privatization: 1999–2011

#### Links with Africa and conflicts in the Arab League



Gaddafi wearing an insignia showing the image of the African continent, 2006

During the final years of the 20th century, Gaddafi—frustrated by the failure of his pan-Arab ideals and the refusal of the Arab world to challenge the international air embargo imposed on Libya—increasingly rejected Arab nationalism in favour of [pan-Africanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Africanism), emphasizing Libya's African identity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski2011142St._John2012227-329][^cite_note-\:8-330] In a 1998 interview, Gaddafi claimed that "the Arab world is finished" and expressed his wish for Libya to become a "black country".[^cite_note-\:8-330] From 1997 to 2000, Libya initiated cooperative agreements or bilateral aid arrangements with 10 African states,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012229-331] and in 1999 joined the [Community of Sahel–Saharan States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_of_Sahel%E2%80%93Saharan_States) (CEN–SAD).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John200899Kawczynski2011189-332] In June 1999, Gaddafi visited Mandela in South Africa,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012226-333] and the next month attended the OAU summit in [Algiers](1644.md), calling for greater political and economic integration across the continent and advocating the foundation of a [United States of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_of_Africa).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012227–228-334] He became one of the founders of the [African Union](10634933.md) (AU), initiated in July 2002 to replace the OAU. At the opening ceremonies, he called for African states to reject conditional aid from the [developed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_country) world, a direct contrast to the message of South African President [Thabo Mbeki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thabo_Mbeki).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski2011190St._John2012229-335] There was speculation that Gaddafi wanted to become the AU's first chair, raising concerns within Africa that this would damage the Union's international standing, particularly with the West.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERamutsindela20093-336]

At the third AU summit, held in Tripoli, Libya, in July 2005, Gaddafi called for greater integration, advocating a single AU passport, a common defence system, and a single currency, using the slogan: "The United States of Africa is the hope."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski2011190–191St._John2012230-337] His proposal for a Union of African States, a project originally conceived by Ghana's [Kwame Nkrumah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kwame_Nkrumah) in the 1960s, was rejected at the 2001 Assembly of Heads of States and Government (AHSG) summit in Lusaka by African leaders who thought it "unrealistic" and "utopian".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin2002280-338] In June 2005, Libya joined the [Common Market for Eastern and Southern Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_Market_for_Eastern_and_Southern_Africa) (COMESA).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012231-339] In March 2008 in Uganda, Gaddafi gave a speech once again urging Africa to reject foreign aid.[^cite_note-340] In August 2008, Gaddafi was proclaimed "[King of Kings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Kings)" by a committee of traditional African [leaders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribal_chief);[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski2011188St._John2012270–271-341] they [crowned](226191.md) him in February 2009, in a ceremony held in [Addis Ababa](52593.md), Ethiopia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski2011190St._John2012272-342] That same month, Gaddafi was elected as the chairperson of the African Union, a position he retained for one year.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERamutsindela20091Kawczynski2011190St._John2012272-343] In October 2010, Gaddafi apologized to African leaders for the historical enslavement of Africans by the [Arab slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_slave_trade).[^cite_note-344]



Gaddafi in 2003

Meanwhile, Gaddafi continued to have testy relationships with most of his fellow Arab leaders. In the 2003 Arab League summit, Gaddafi was involved in a public verbal altercation with [Abdullah of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_of_Saudi_Arabia), then the Crown Prince. Gaddafi accused Saudi Arabia of having made an "alliance with the devil" when it invited the US to intervene in the 1991 [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War). Abdullah responded that Gaddafi was a "liar" and an "agent of colonizers" and threatened Gaddafi that "your grave awaits you."[^cite_note-345] Two weeks after the summit, Gaddafi allegedly plotted with the Emir [Hamad bin Khalifa Al Thani](295929.md) of [Qatar](8238258.md) to assassinate Abdullah.[^cite_note-346][^cite_note-347][^cite_note-\:1-348] The plot was overseen by Libyan intelligence chief [Moussa Koussa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moussa_Koussa), Mohammed Ismail (a colonel in Gaddafi's military intelligence), and [Abdul Rahman al-Amoudi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Rahman_al-Amoudi) (an American citizen and founder of [American Muslim Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Muslim_Council)). The assassination conspiracy was foiled by Saudi intelligence with the help of the [FBI](11127.md) and [CIA](5183633.md).[^cite_note-349] Amoudi was sentenced to 23 years in prison in the US and stripped of his American citizenship. Ismail was arrested by Saudi Arabia, pardoned by Abdullah in 2005, and later acquired [UAE](69328.md) citizenship due to his close ties with its ruler [Mohamed bin Zayed Al Nahyan](2121945.md).[^cite_note-\:1-348] After the failure of the assassination plot, Gaddafi continued to discuss instigating a [regime change](154074.md) in Saudi Arabia with multiple [power brokers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_broker) in the [Persian Gulf](24761.md), including Qatar's Foreign Minister [Hamad bin Jassim bin Jaber Al Thani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamad_bin_Jassim_bin_Jaber_Al_Thani), [Oman](22316.md)'s foreign minister [Yusuf bin Alawi bin Abdullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yusuf_bin_Alawi_bin_Abdullah), and Kuwaiti extremist preacher Hakem al-Mutairi.[^cite_note-350][^cite_note-351][^cite_note-352]

The Gaddafi–Abdullah feud came into public view again in the [2009 Arab League summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Arab_League_summit) when Gaddafi accused Abdullah, who had become [King of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Saudi_Arabia) in 2005, of being created by Britain and protected by the US.[^cite_note-353] Alluding to their 2003 altercation, Gaddafi taunted Abdullah for ostensibly avoiding a confrontation with him for six years and quoted Abdullah's 2003 "grave awaits you" threat back at him before storming out of the meeting to visit a museum.[^cite_note-354] Abdullah also left the meeting hall in anger.[^cite_note-355] A Saudi official later claimed that Gaddafi and Abdullah had held a 30 minutes meeting at the sideline of the summit and that the "personal problem" between them was "over".[^cite_note-356] However, Gaddafi had given weapons and money to the [Houthis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houthi_movement) to attack Saudi Arabia.[^cite_note-357]

#### Rebuilding links with the West

In 1999, Libya began secret talks with the British government to normalize relations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle2008a215-358] In 2001, Gaddafi publicly condemned the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md) on the US by [al-Qaeda](1921.md), expressing sympathy with the victims and calling for Libyan involvement in the US-led [war on terror](13425800.md) against militant Islamism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle2008a220St._John2008101Kawczynski2011176St._John2012243-359] His government continued suppressing domestic Islamism, at the same time as Gaddafi called for the wider application of *sharia* law.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012254-360] Libya also cemented connections with China and North Korea, being visited by Chinese President [Jiang Zemin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jiang_Zemin) in April 2002.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012235-361] However, relations with China became strained in May 2006 due to a visit to Tripoli by Taiwanese President [Chen Shui-bian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chen_Shui-bian).[^cite_note-362][^cite_note-363][^cite_note-364] Influenced by the events of the [Iraq War](5043324.md), in December 2003, Libya renounced its possession of [weapons of mass destruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weapon_of_mass_destruction), [decommissioning its chemical and nuclear weapons programs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disarmament_of_Libya).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle20068Vandewalle2008a217Kawczynski2011162,_184St._John2012244Kamel2016694-365] Relations with the US improved as a result.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski2011178–179St._John2012245-366] British Prime Minister [Tony Blair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Blair) visited Gaddafi in March 2004;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski2011240–241St._John2012240–241-367] the pair developed close personal ties.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZoubir2009412-368] In 2003, Libya paid US\$2.7 billion to the families of the victims of the Lockerbie bombing as it was the condition the US and UK had made for terminating the remaining UN sanctions. Libya continued to deny any role in the bombing.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoyd-Judson200591St._John2008101-369][^cite_note-370] In 2009, Gaddafi attempted to strong-arm global energy companies operating in Libya to cover Libya's settlement with the families of the victims of Lockerbie.[^cite_note-\:10-371]

Video showing the meeting with Russian Prime Minister [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) and Muammar Gaddafi, in 2008

In 2004, Gaddafi traveled to the headquarters of the [European Union](9317.md) (EU) in [Brussels](3708.md)—signifying improved relations between Libya and the EU—and the EU dropped its sanctions on Libya.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski2011175St._John2008101St._John2012237-372] As a strategic player in Europe's attempts to stem illegal migration from Africa,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZoubir2009408-373] in October 2010, the EU paid Libya over €50 million to stop African migrants passing into Europe; Gaddafi encouraged the move, saying that it was necessary to prevent the loss of European cultural identity to a new "Black Europe".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012274Kamel2016684-374] Gaddafi also completed agreements with the Italian government that they would invest in various infrastructure projects as reparations for past Italian colonial policies in Libya.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZoubir2009410-375] Italian Prime Minister [Silvio Berlusconi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silvio_Berlusconi) gave Libya an official apology in 2006, after which Gaddafi called him the "iron man" for his courage in doing so.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZoubir2009410–411-376] In August 2008, Gaddafi and Berlusconi signed a historic cooperation [treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty) in [Benghazi](204114.md);[^cite_note-treaty-377][^cite_note-ansa-378] under its terms, Italy would pay \$5 billion to Libya as compensation for its former [military occupation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Libya). In exchange, Libya would take measures to combat [illegal immigration](7124412.md) coming from its shores and boost investment in Italian companies.[^cite_note-ansa-378][^cite_note-rep-379]

After the US removed Libya from its list of state sponsors of terrorism in 2006,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski2011176-380] Gaddafi nevertheless continued his anti-Western rhetoric. At the [2008 Arab League summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Arab_League_summit), held in Syria, he warned fellow Arab leaders that they could be overthrown and executed by the US like [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein).[^cite_note-381][^cite_note-382] At the [Second Africa-South America Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Africa-South_America_Summit), held in Venezuela in September 2009, he called for a military alliance across Africa and Latin America to rival NATO.[^cite_note-383] That same month he traveled to New York City and addressed the [United Nations General Assembly](31957.md) for the first time on 23 September 2009, using it to condemn "Western aggression", and spoke for just over 90 minutes instead of the allotted 15.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012276-384][^cite_note-385] In the spring of 2010, Gaddafi proclaimed *jihad* against Switzerland after Swiss police accused two of his family members of criminal activity in the country, resulting in the breakdown of bilateral relations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012274-386]

Gaddafi allegedly financed [Nicolas Sarkozy](557007.md) in the [2007 French presidential election](11105698.md).[^cite_note-387][^cite_note-388][^cite_note-389][^cite_note-390] He also financed Austrian far-right politician [Jörg Haider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%B6rg_Haider) starting in 2000.[^cite_note-391][^cite_note-392][^cite_note-393]

As revealed by documents found in [Moussa Koussa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moussa_Koussa)'s Tripoli office, the CIA and MI6 both extradited terrorism suspects to Libya from 2002 to 2004,[^cite_note-394] with the CIA having sent suspects for questioning to Libya at least 8 times, despite the nation's reputation for torture.[^cite_note-395] A 2012 [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) report conducted interviews with 14 former Libyan detainees, mostly members of the anti-Gaddafi Libyan Islamic Fighting Group, who testified to being tortured at CIA prisons through methods such as waterboarding before their extradition to Libya, followed by torture in Libyan prisons by beating and use of electric shocks.[^cite_note-396][^cite_note-397] The leader of the Libyan Islamist Fighting Group, [Abdel-Hakim Belhaj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdelhakim_Belhaj), stated that while being tortured in [Abu Salim prison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Salim_prison), he was forced to give information regarding Libyans living in the United Kingdom, who were then allegedly arbitrarily arrested by the British government.[^cite_note-398]

#### Economic reform

Libya's economy witnessed increasing [privatization](24661.md); although rejecting the socialist policies of nationalized industry advocated in *The Green Book*, government figures asserted that they were forging "people's socialism" rather than capitalism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012250-399] Gaddafi welcomed these reforms, calling for wide-scale privatization in a March 2003 speech;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle2008a224-400] he promised that Libya would join the [World Trade Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2008101–102-401] These reforms encouraged private investment in Libya's economy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKamel2016697-402] By 2004, there was US\$40 billion of direct foreign investment in Libya, a six-fold rise over 2003.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski2011180St._John2012248-403] Sectors of Libya's population reacted against these reforms with public demonstrations,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012248-404] and in March 2006, revolutionary hard-liners took control of the GPC cabinet; although scaling back the pace of the changes, they did not halt them.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle2008a228St._John2012249–250-405] In 2010, plans were announced that would have seen half the Libyan economy privatized over the next decade,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012263–264-406] these plans appear to have been soon abandoned however, as the companies that the government stated they were going to float on the stock market, among them the National Commercial Bank and the [Libyan Iron and Steel Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libyan_Iron_and_Steel_Company) were never floated and remained 100% state-owned. Many socialist policies remained however, with subsidiaries of logistics company HB Group being nationalized in 2007.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECooley1983467–489BlundyLycett1987156SacerdotiAcconci2011312–313-407] Agriculture remained largely untouched by the reforms, with farms remaining cooperatives, the [Agricultural Bank of Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural_Bank_of_Libya) remaining wholly state-owned and state interventionist policies and price controls remaining.[^cite_note-408] The oil industry remained largely state-owned, with the wholly state-owned [National Oil Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Oil_Corporation) retaining a 70% share in Libya's oil industry, the government also imposed a 93% tax on all oil that foreign companies produced in Libya.[^cite_note-409] Price controls and subsidies over oil and food remained in place, and state-provided benefits such as free education, universal healthcare, free housing, free water and free electricity remained in place.[^cite_note-410] Libya also changed its stance on the WTO after the removal of technocrat [Shukri Ghanem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shukri_Ghanem), with Gaddafi condemning the WTO as a neocolonial terrorist organisation, and urging African and Third World countries not to join it.[^cite_note-411]

While there was no accompanying political liberalization, with Gaddafi retaining predominant control,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle2008a231-412] in March 2010, the government devolved further powers to the municipal councils.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012257-413] Rising numbers of reformist technocrats attained positions in the country's governance; best known was Gaddafi's son and heir apparent [Saif al-Islam Gaddafi](1196902.md), who was openly critical of Libya's human rights record. He led a group who proposed the drafting of a new constitution, although it was never adopted.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle2008a225St._John2012249–269-414] Involved in encouraging tourism, Saif founded several privately run media channels in 2008, but after criticizing the government, they were nationalized in 2009.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski2011216,_227–228-415]

## Libyan civil war and death

Main article: [Libyan civil war (2011)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libyan_civil_war_\(2011\))

Further information: [2011 military intervention in Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_military_intervention_in_Libya)

### Origins and development: February–August 2011



An anti-Gaddafist placard being displayed by demonstrators in Ireland in 2011.

After the start of the [Arab Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Spring), in 2011, Gaddafi spoke out in favour of Tunisian President [Zine El Abidine Ben Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zine_El_Abidine_Ben_Ali), then threatened by the [Tunisian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tunisian_Revolution). He suggested that Tunisia's people would be satisfied if Ben Ali introduced a *jamahiriyah* system there.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012278-416] Fearing domestic protest, Libya's government implemented preventive measures by reducing [food prices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_prices), purging the army leadership of potential defectors, and releasing several Islamist prisoners.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012282–283-417] This proved ineffective, and on 17 February 2011, major protests broke out against Gaddafi's government. Unlike Tunisia or Egypt, Libya was largely religiously homogeneous and had no strong Islamist movement, but there was widespread dissatisfaction with the corruption and entrenched systems of patronage, while unemployment had reached around 30 percent.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski2011231St._John2012279–281-418]

Accusing the rebels of being "drugged" and linked to al-Qaeda, Gaddafi proclaimed that he would die a [martyr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martyr) rather than leave Libya.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski2011242-419] As he announced that the rebels would be "hunted down street by street, house by house and wardrobe by wardrobe",[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski2011242–243-420] the army opened fire on protesters in Benghazi, killing hundreds.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012283-421] Shocked at the government's response, a number of senior politicians resigned or defected to the protesters' side.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012284Vandewalle2008a236-422] The uprising spread quickly through Libya's less economically developed eastern half.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle2008a236-423] By February's end, eastern cities such as Benghazi, Misrata, al-Bayda, and Tobruk were controlled by rebels,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012284-424] and the Benghazi-based [National Transitional Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Transitional_Council) (NTC) formed to represent them.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012286Human_Rights_Watch201216-425]



Pro-Gaddafi protests in Tripoli, May 2011

In the conflict's early months it appeared that Gaddafi's government—with its greater fire-power—would be victorious.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle2008a236-423] Both sides disregarded the [laws of war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laws_of_war), committing human rights abuses, including arbitrary arrests, [torture](47702.md), [extrajudicial executions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extrajudicial_killing), and revenge attacks.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHuman_Rights_Watch201217–18-426] On 26 February, the [United Nations Security Council](31956.md) passed [Resolution 1970](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_1970), suspending Libya from the [UN Human Rights Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Human_Rights_Council), implementing sanctions and calling for an [International Criminal Court](14880.md) (ICC) investigation into the killing of unarmed civilians.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESacerdotiAcconci201161–62St._John2012284Human_Rights_Watch201216-427] In March, the Security Council declared a [no-fly zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_1973) to protect the civilian population from aerial bombardment, calling on foreign nations to enforce it; it also specifically prohibited foreign occupation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle2008a236Human_Rights_Watch201216-428] Ignoring this, Qatar sent hundreds of troops to support the dissidents and, along with France and the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md), provided weaponry and military training to the NTC.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHuman_Rights_Watch201216-429] NATO announced that it would enforce the no-fly zone.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle2008a236St._John2012284-430] On 30 April a NATO airstrike killed [Gaddafi's sixth son](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saif_al-Arab_Gaddafi) and three of his grandsons in Tripoli.[^cite_note-431] This Western military intervention was criticized by various leftist governments, including those that had criticized Gaddafi's response to the protests, because they regarded it as an imperialist attempt to secure control of Libya's resources.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECastro2011308–309-432]

In June, the ICC issued arrest warrants for Gaddafi, his son Saif al-Islam, and his brother-in-law [Abdullah Senussi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_Senussi), head of state security, for charges concerning crimes against humanity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski2011257St._John2012286-433] That month, [Amnesty International](18947898.md) published their report, finding that Gaddafi's forces were responsible for numerous war crimes but added that a number of allegations of human rights abuses lacked credible evidence. The report added that "much Western media coverage has from the outset presented a very one-sided view of the logic of events, portraying the protest movement as entirely peaceful and repeatedly suggesting that the regime's security forces were unaccountably massacring unarmed demonstrators".[^cite_note-AmnestyRape-434] In July, over 30 governments recognized the NTC as the legitimate government of Libya; Gaddafi called on his supporters to "Trample on those recognitions, trample on them under your feet ... They are worthless".[^cite_note-INDtncofficialgov-2] In August, the [Arab League](52625.md) recognized the NTC as "the legitimate representative of the Libyan state".[^cite_note-taipeitimmes20110826-4]

Aided by NATO air cover, the rebel militia pushed westward, defeating loyalist armies and securing control of the centre of the country.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012285-435] Gaining the support of [Amazigh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amazigh) ([Berber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berberism)) communities of the [Nafusa Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nafusa_Mountains), who had long been persecuted as non-Arabic speakers under Gaddafi, the NTC armies surrounded Gaddafi loyalists in several key areas of western Libya.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012285-435] In August, the rebels seized [Zliten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zliten) and [Tripoli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Tripoli_\(2011\)), ending the last vestiges of Gaddafist power.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012286-436] It is probable that without the NATO air strikes supporting the rebels, they would not have been able to advance west and Gaddafi's forces would have ultimately retaken control of eastern Libya.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPargeter20128-437]

### Capture and killing

Main articles: [Killing of Muammar Gaddafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killing_of_Muammar_Gaddafi) and [International reactions to the killing of Muammar Gaddafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_reactions_to_the_killing_of_Muammar_Gaddafi)

After the [fall of Tripoli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Tripoli_\(2011\)) in August 2011, only a few towns in western Libya such as [Bani Walid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bani_Walid), [Sebha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabha,_Libya), and [Sirte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sirte) remained Gaddafist strongholds.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012286-436] Gaddafi was reportedly planning to catch up with his Sebha commander [Ali Kanna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Kanna)'s [Tuareg](103054.md) forces and seek asylum in [Burkina Faso](3470.md).[^cite_note-438] Instead, Gaddafi retreated to his hometown of Sirte,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHuman_Rights_Watch201220-439] where he convened a meeting with his son [Mutassim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mutassim_Gaddafi) and intelligence chief [Abdullah Senussi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_Senussi) and learned that his youngest son [Khamis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khamis_Gaddafi) had been killed by a NATO airstrike on 29 August.[^cite_note-\:9-440] In the weeks that followed, Gaddafi continued to broadcast defiant audio messages through Syria-based [Arrai TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrai_TV).[^cite_note-441][^cite_note-442][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012286-436] On 10 September, General Massoud Abdel Hafiz announced the formation of the Republic of [Fezzan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fezzan) in Sebha, where Gaddafi would be president for life.[^cite_note-443] Sebha fell on 22 September.[^cite_note-444]

Surrounding himself with bodyguards and a small entourage,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHuman_Rights_Watch201220-439] including Mutassim, security chief [Mansour Dhao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mansour_Dhao), and defense minister [Abu-Bakr Yunis Jabr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu-Bakr_Yunis_Jabr), Gaddafi continually changed residences to escape NATO and NTC shelling, devoting his days to prayer and reading the Qur'an.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHuman_Rights_Watch201221–22-445][^cite_note-446][^cite_note-\:9-440] On 20 October, Gaddafi recorded a farewell audio message for his family, later publicized by [Al-Hadath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AlHadath), and then broke out of Sirte's District 2 in a joint civilian-military convoy.[^cite_note-447][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHuman_Rights_Watch201223-448][^cite_note-BBC20112-1][^cite_note-449][^cite_note-450] According to Dhao, it was a "suicide mission" as Gaddafi wanted to die in the Jarref Valley, close to where he was born.[^cite_note-451][^cite_note-452] At around 08\:30, NATO bombers attacked, destroying at least 14 vehicles and killing at least 53 people.[^cite_note-BBC20112-1][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHuman_Rights_Watch201224–25-453] The convoy scattered, and Gaddafi and those closest to him fled to a nearby villa, which was shelled by rebel militia from Misrata. Fleeing to a construction site, Gaddafi and his inner cohort hid inside drainage pipes while his bodyguards battled the rebels; in the conflict, Gaddafi suffered head injuries from a grenade blast while Jabr was killed.[^cite_note-BBC20112-1][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHuman_Rights_Watch201226–27-454] The Misrata militia took Gaddafi prisoner, causing serious injuries as they tried to apprehend him; the events were filmed on a mobile phone. A video appears to picture Gaddafi being poked or stabbed in the anus "with some kind of stick or knife"[^cite_note-455] or possibly a bayonet.[^cite_note-Irish_Times-456][^cite_note-457] Pulled onto the front of a pick-up truck, he fell off as it drove away. His semi-naked body was then placed into an ambulance and taken to [Misrata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misrata); upon arrival, he was found to be dead.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHuman_Rights_Watch201228–29-458] Official NTC accounts claimed that Gaddafi was caught in a crossfire and died from bullet wounds. Other eye-witness accounts claimed that rebels had fatally shot Gaddafi in the stomach.[^cite_note-BBC20112-1]

That afternoon, NTC Prime Minister [Mahmoud Jibril](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmoud_Jibril) publicly revealed the news of Gaddafi's death.[^cite_note-BBC20112-1] His corpse was placed in the freezer of a local market alongside the corpses of Yunis Jabr and Mutassim; the bodies were publicly displayed for four days, with Libyans from all over the country coming to view them.[^cite_note-459] Footage of Gaddafi's death was broadcast extensively across media networks internationally.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarnielLavie-DinurAzran2015171,_176-460] In response to international calls, on 24 October Jibril announced that a commission would investigate Gaddafi's death.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHuman_Rights_Watch201244-461] On 25 October, the NTC announced that Gaddafi had been buried at an unidentified location in the desert.[^cite_note-462]

## Political ideology

Main article: [Third International Theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_International_Theory)

> We call it the Third \[International] Theory to indicate that there is a new path for all those who reject both materialist capitalism and atheist communism. The path is for all the people of the world who abhor the dangerous confrontation between the Warsaw and North Atlantic military alliances. It is for all those who believe that all nations of the world are brothers under the aegis of the rule of God.

— Muammar Gaddafi, 1973

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198787-463]

Gaddafi's ideological worldview was molded by his environment, namely his Islamic faith, his Bedouin upbringing, and his disgust at the actions of Italian colonialists in Libya.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198643-464] As a schoolboy, Gaddafi adopted the ideologies of [Arab nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_nationalism) and [Arab socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_socialism), influenced in particular by [Nasserism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasserism), the thought of the Egyptian President Nasser, whom Gaddafi regarded as his hero;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198643BlundyLycett198718-465] Nasser privately described Gaddafi as "a nice boy, but terribly naïve".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198718-97] During the early 1970s, Gaddafi formulated his own particular approach to Arab nationalism and socialism, known as [Third International Theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_International_Theory), which *[The New York Times](30680.md)* described as a combination of "[utopian socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utopian_socialism), Arab nationalism, and the Third World revolutionary theory that was in vogue at the time".[^cite_note-nytimes_green-466] In addition to Nasser, Gaddafi also cited [Charles de Gaulle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_de_Gaulle), [Sun Yat-sen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sun_Yat-sen), [Abraham Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln) and [Josip Broz Tito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josip_Broz_Tito) as political inspirations.[^cite_note-467] He regarded this system as a practical alternative to the then-dominant international models of Western capitalism and [Marxism–Leninism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxism%E2%80%93Leninism).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John198728-468] He laid out the principles of this Theory in the three volumes of *The Green Book*, in which he sought to "explain the structure of the ideal society".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198657-469]

The Libyan studies specialist Ronald Bruce St. John regarded Arab nationalism as Gaddafi's "primordial value",[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John1983473St._John198721St._John200892-470] stating that during the early years of his government, Gaddafi was "the Arab nationalist par excellence".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John198726-471] Gaddafi called for the Arab world to regain its dignity and assert a major place on the world stage, blaming Arab backwardness on stagnation resulting from [Ottoman](22278.md) rule, European colonialism and imperialism, and corrupt and repressive monarchies.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John1983474St._John198726–27-472] Gaddafi's Arab nationalist views led him to the [pan-Arabist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Arabist) belief in the need for unity across the Arab world, combining the Arab nation under a single nation-state.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198659St._John198719,_49-473] To this end, he had proposed a political union with five neighbouring Arab states by 1974, although without success.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John198758-474] In keeping with his views regarding Arabs, his political stance was described as [nativist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nativism_\(politics\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch198463-475] Gaddafi also had international ambitions, wanting to export his revolutionary ideas throughout the world.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaynes199059Pargeter20123-476] Gaddafi saw his socialist *Jamahiriyah* as a model for the Arab, Islamic, and non-aligned worlds to follow,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198654-477] and in his speeches declared that his Third International Theory would eventually guide the entire planet.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John198734-478] He nevertheless had minimal success in exporting the ideology outside of Libya.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John198729-479]

Along with Arab nationalism, anti-imperialism was also a defining feature of Gaddafi's regime during its early years. He believed in opposing Western imperialism and colonialism in the Arab world, including any Western expansionism through the form of Israel.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZoubir2009402-480] He offered support to a broad range of political groups abroad that called themselves "anti-imperialist", especially those that set themselves in opposition to the United States.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaynes199060-481] For many years, [anti-Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Zionism) was a fundamental component of Gaddafi's ideology. He believed that the state of Israel should not exist and that any Arab compromise with the Israeli government was a betrayal of the Arab people.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198654BlundyLycett198718-482] In large part due to their support of Israel, Gaddafi despised the United States, considering the country to be [imperialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperialism) and lambasting it as "the embodiment of evil".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198719,_197-483] He sought to distinguish "oriental" Jews who had lived in the Middle East for generations from the European Jews who had migrated to Palestine during the 20th century, calling the latter "vagabonds" and "mercenaries" who should return to Europe.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John1983478-484] He rallied against Jews in many of his speeches, with [David Blundy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Blundy) and [Andrew Lycett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Lycett) claiming that his [antisemitism](1078.md) was "almost [Hitlerian](2731583.md)".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198725-485] As Pan-Africanism increasingly became his focus in the early 21st century, Gaddafi became less interested in the Israel-Palestine issue, calling for the two communities to form a new [single-state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One-state_solution) that he termed "[Isratin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isratin)".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2008100-486][^cite_note-487] This would have led the Jewish population to become a minority within the new state.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarnielLavie-DinurAzran2015177-488]

### Islamic modernism and Islamic socialism

Gaddafi rejected the [secularist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism) approach to Arab nationalism that had been pervasive in Syria,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John198730-489] with his revolutionary movement placing a far stronger emphasis on Islam than previous Arab nationalist movements had done.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John1983473-490] He deemed Arabism and Islam to be inseparable, referring to them as "one and indivisible",[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986161-491] and called on the Arab world's Christian minority to convert to Islam.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John1983476Bearman1986161St._John198730-492] He insisted that Islamic law should be the basis for the law of the state, blurring any distinction between the religious and secular realms.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFirst197425-493] He desired unity across the Islamic world,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198719-494] and encouraged the propagation of the faith elsewhere; on a 2010 visit to Italy, he paid a modelling agency to find 200 young Italian women for a lecture he gave urging them to convert.[^cite_note-495] According to the Gaddafi biographer Jonathan Bearman, in Islamic terms Gaddafi was a [modernist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Modernism) rather than a [fundamentalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_fundamentalism), for he subordinated religion to the political system rather than seeking to Islamicise the state as Islamists sought to do.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986164-496] He was driven by a sense of "divine mission", believing himself a conduit of God's will, and thought that he must achieve his goals "no matter what the cost".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198645,_50-497] His interpretation of Islam was nevertheless idiosyncratic,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986164-496] and he clashed with conservative Libyan clerics. Many criticized his attempts to encourage women to enter traditionally male-only sectors of society, such as the armed forces. Gaddafi was keen to improve women's status, although saw the sexes as "separate but equal" and therefore felt women should usually remain in traditional roles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198633,_53-498]

> The purpose of the socialist society is the happiness of man, which can only be realized through material and spiritual freedom. Attainment of such freedom depends on the extent of man's ownership of his needs; ownership that is personal and sacredly guaranteed, i.e. your needs must neither be owned by somebody else, nor subject to plunder by any part of society.

— Muammar Gaddafi

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986157-499]

Gaddafi described his approach to economics as "Islamic socialism".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFirst1974255St._John1983482Harris198648-500] For him, a socialist society could be defined as one in which men controlled their own needs, either through personal ownership or through a collective.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986157-499] Although the early policies pursued by his government were state capitalist in orientation, by 1978 he believed that private ownership of the means of production was exploitative and thus he sought to move Libya away from capitalism and towards socialism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch198469-501] Private enterprise was largely eliminated in favour of a centrally controlled economy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John1983484-502] The extent to which Libya became socialist under Gaddafi is disputed. Bearman suggested that while Libya did undergo "a profound social revolution", he did not think that "a socialist society" was established in Libya.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986xvii-503] Conversely, St. John expressed the view that "if socialism is defined as a redistribution of wealth and resources, a socialist revolution clearly occurred in Libya" under Gaddafi's regime.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John2012173-208]

Gaddafi was staunchly [anti-Marxist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Marxism),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986104-504] and in 1973 declared that "it is the duty of every Muslim to combat" Marxism because it promotes atheism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986105-505] In his view, ideologies like Marxism and Zionism were alien to the Islamic world and were a threat to the [ummah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ummah), or global Islamic community.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986104–105-506] Nevertheless, Blundy and Lycett noted that Gaddafi's socialism had a "curiously Marxist undertone",[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198798-507] with political scientist Sami Hajjar arguing that Gaddafi's model of socialism offered a simplification of [Karl Marx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Marx) and [Friedrich Engels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Engels)'s theories.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHajjar1982-508] While acknowledging the Marxist influence on Gaddafi's thought, Bearman stated that the Libyan leader rejected Marxism's core tenet, that of [class struggle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Class_struggle) as the main engine of social development.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986158-509] Instead of embracing the Marxist idea that a socialist society emerged from class struggle between the [proletariat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proletariat) and [bourgeoisie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bourgeoisie), Gaddafi believed that socialism would be achieved through overturning "unnatural" capitalism and returning society to its "natural equilibrium".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986158-509] In this, he sought to replace a capitalist economy with one based on his own romanticized ideas of a traditional, pre-capitalist past.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986159-510] This owed much to the Islamic belief in [God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Islam)'s natural law providing order to the universe.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986160-511]

## Personal life

Main article: [Personal life of Muammar Gaddafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personal_life_of_Muammar_Gaddafi)



Gaddafi (right) with Nimeiry and Nasser in 1969

A very private individual,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198643-464] Gaddafi was given to rumination and solitude and could be reclusive.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986284-512] Gaddafi described himself as a "simple revolutionary" and "pious Muslim" called upon by God to continue Nasser's work.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198648-513] Gaddafi was an austere and devout Muslim,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John1987145Vandewalle20066-514] although, according to Vandewalle, his interpretation of Islam was "deeply personal and idiosyncratic".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle20066-210] He was also a [football](10568.md) enthusiast[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198722-515] and enjoyed both playing that game and horse-riding as a means of recreation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986285-516] He regarded himself as an intellectual;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPargeter20122-517] he was a fan of [Beethoven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ludwig_van_Beethoven) and said his favourite novels were *[Uncle Tom's Cabin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncle_Tom's_Cabin)*, *[Roots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roots:_The_Saga_of_an_American_Family)*, and *[The Outsider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Outsider_\(Wilson_book\))*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198722-515]

Gaddafi considered personal appearance important;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986285-516] Blundy and Lycett described him as "extraordinarily vain".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198724-518] Gaddafi had a large wardrobe, and sometimes changed his outfit more than once a day.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198724-518] He favoured either a military uniform or traditional Libyan dress, tending to eschew Western-style suits.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986285-516] He saw himself as a fashion icon, stating "Whatever I wear becomes a fad. I wear a certain shirt and suddenly everyone is wearing it."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198724-518] After his ascension to power, Gaddafi moved to the [Bab al-Azizia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bab_al-Azizia) barracks, a 6-square-kilometre (2.3 square miles) fortified compound, 3.2 kilometres (2 miles) from the centre of Tripoli.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett19871-519] In the 1980s, his lifestyle was considered modest in comparison to those of many other Arab leaders.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198732-520]

He was preoccupied with his own security, regularly changing where he slept and sometimes grounding all other planes in Libya when he was flying.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198650-202] He made particular requests when travelling to foreign countries. During his trips to Rome, Paris, Madrid, Moscow, and New York City,[^cite_note-abcnews-521][^cite_note-522] he lived in a bulletproof tent, following his Bedouin traditions.[^cite_note-abcnews-521][^cite_note-523] Gaddafi was confrontational in his approach to foreign powers[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198651-524] and generally shunned Western ambassadors and diplomats, believing them to be spies.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198721-525]



Gaddafi with Spanish Prime Minister [José Luis Rodríguez Zapatero](19614411.md) in 2010

In the 1970s and 1980s, there were reports of his making sexual advances toward female reporters and members of his entourage.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198653–54BlundyLycett198722–23-526] Starting in the 1980s, he travelled with his all-female [Amazonian Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amazonian_Guard), who were allegedly sworn to a life of celibacy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett1987112-527] After Gaddafi's death, a Libyan psychologist, [Seham Sergewa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seham_Sergewa), part of a team investigating sexual offences during the civil war, stated that five of the guards told her they had been raped by Gaddafi and senior officials.[^cite_note-rape-528] After Gaddafi's death, a French journalist, [Annick Cojean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annick_Cojean?action=edit\&redlink=1), published a book alleging that Gaddafi had had sexual relations with women, some in their early teenage years, who had been specially selected for him.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECojean2013-529] One of those Cojean interviewed, a woman named Soraya, claimed that Gaddafi kept her imprisoned in a basement for six years, where he repeatedly raped her; urinated on her; and forced her to watch pornography, drink alcohol, and snort cocaine.[^cite_note-harem-530] The sexual abuse was said to have been facilitated by Gaddafi's Chief of Protocol [Nuri al-Mismari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuri_al-Mismari) and Mabrouka Sherif.[^cite_note-531][^cite_note-532] Gaddafi also hired several Ukrainian nurses to care for him; one described him as kind and considerate and was surprised that allegations of abuse had been made against him.[^cite_note-533]

&#x20;Gaddafi married his first wife, Fatiha al-Nuri, in 1969. They had one son, [Muhammad Gaddafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Gaddafi) (born 1970); their relationship was strained, and they divorced in 1970.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198653BlundyLycett198722-534] Gaddafi's second wife was [Safia Farkash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safia_Farkash), née el-Brasai, a former nurse from the Obeidat tribe, born in [Bayda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayda,_Libya).[^cite_note-535] They met in 1969, after his ascension to power, when he was hospitalized with appendicitis; he claimed that it was love at first sight.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris198653BlundyLycett198722-534] The couple remained married until his death. Together they had seven biological children:[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986285-516] [Saif al-Islam Gaddafi](1196902.md) (1972–2026), [Al-Saadi Gaddafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Saadi_Gaddafi) (born 1973), [Mutassim Gaddafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mutassim_Gaddafi) (1974–2011), [Hannibal Muammar Gaddafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hannibal_Muammar_Gaddafi) (born 1975), [Aisha Gaddafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aisha_Gaddafi) (born 1976), [Saif al-Arab Gaddafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saif_al-Arab_Gaddafi) (1982–2011), and [Khamis Gaddafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khamis_Gaddafi) (1983–2011). He also adopted two children, [Hana Gaddafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hana_Gaddafi) and Milad Gaddafi.[^cite_note-536] Several of his sons gained a reputation for lavish and anti-social behaviour in Libya, which proved a source of resentment toward his administration.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPargeter20126-537] At least three of his cousins were prominent figures in Gaddafi's regime. [Ahmed Gaddaf al-Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmed_Gaddaf_al-Dam) is Libya's former Special Envoy to Egypt and a leading figure of the Gaddafi regime.[^cite_note-538] [Mansour Dhao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mansour_Dhao) was his chief of security and led the [People's Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Guard_\(Libya\)).[^cite_note-539] [Sayyid Gaddaf al-Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sayyid_Gaddaf_al-Dam) was a brigadier general and described as the second most powerful person in Libya in the 1980s.[^cite_note-540][^cite_note-541]

Saif al-Islam Gaddafi, the son who was considered to be Gaddafi's main heir, [was assassinated](82317452.md) in February 2026.[^cite_note-542][^cite_note-543]

## Public life



13th Anniversary of 1 September Revolution on postage stamp, Libya 1982

According to Vandewalle, Gaddafi "dominated \[Libya's] political life" during his period in power.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVandewalle20065-544] The sociologist Raymond A. Hinnebusch described the Libyan as "perhaps the most exemplary contemporary case of the politics of charismatic leadership", displaying all of the traits of [charismatic authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charismatic_authority) outlined by the sociologist [Max Weber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Max_Weber).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch198459-545] According to Hinnebusch, the foundations of Gaddafi's "personal charismatic authority" in Libya stemmed from the blessing he had received from Nasser coupled with "nationalist achievements" such as the expulsion of foreign military bases, the extraction of higher prices for Libyan oil, and his vocal support for the Palestinian and anti-imperialist causes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch198462-546]

A [cult of personality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cult_of_personality) devoted to Gaddafi existed in Libya through most of his rule.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986283BlundyLycett198720-547] His biographer Alison Pargeter noted that "he filled every space, moulding the entire country around himself."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPargeter20122-517] Depictions of his face could be found throughout the country, including on postage stamps, watches, and school satchels.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198720-548] Quotations from *The Green Book* appeared on a wide variety of places, from street walls to airports and pens, and were put to pop music for public release.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198720-548] In private, Gaddafi often complained that he disliked this personality cult surrounding him, but that he tolerated it because the people of Libya adored him.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198720-548] The cult served a political purpose, with Gaddafi helping to provide a central identity for the Libyan state.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBearman1986284-512]

Several biographers and observers characterized Gaddafi as a [populist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populism).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHinnebusch198461BlundyLycett198720-549] He enjoyed attending lengthy, often televised, public sessions where he was questioned.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFirst197422–23-550] Throughout Libya, crowds of supporters arrived at public events where he appeared. Described as "spontaneous demonstrations" by the government, groups were often coerced or paid to attend.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198716-551] He was typically late to public events, sometimes failing to arrive.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198717-552] Although Bianco thought he had a "gift for oratory",[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBianco19757-553] he was considered a poor orator by Blundy and Lycett.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198718-97] The biographer [Daniel Kawczynski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Kawczynski) noted that Gaddafi was famed for his "lengthy, wandering" speeches,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski2011191-554] which typically involved criticizing Israel and the US.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198717-552] The journalist [Ruth First](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruth_First) called his speeches "an inexhaustible flow; didactic, at times incoherent; peppered with snatches of half-formed opinions; admonitions; confidences; some sound common sense, and as much prejudice".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFirst197423-555]

## Reception and legacy

Main article: [Reception and legacy of Muammar Gaddafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reception_and_legacy_of_Muammar_Gaddafi)



10th anniversary of Great Libyan Revolution coin

Supporters praised Gaddafi's administration for the creation of a more equal society through domestic reform.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198719Kawczynski2011196–200-556] They stressed the regime's achievements in combating homelessness, ensuring access to food and safe drinking water, and to dramatic improvements in education.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198719Kawczynski2011196–200-556] Supporters have also applauded achievements in medical care, praising the universal free healthcare provided under the Gaddafist administration, with diseases like [cholera](7591.md) and [typhoid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Typhoid) being contained and life expectancy raised.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlundyLycett198719Kawczynski2011196–200-556]

Gaddafi's government's treatment of non-Arab Libyans came in for criticism from human rights activists, with native Berbers, Italian colonists, Jews, refugees, and foreign workers all facing persecution in Gaddafist Libya.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKawczynski2011202–203,_209-557] Human rights groups also criticized the treatment of migrants, including [asylum seekers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asylum_seekers), who passed through Gaddafi's Libya on their way to Europe.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZoubir2009409-558] During the Civil War, various leftist groups endorsed the anti-Gaddafist rebels—but not the Western military intervention—by arguing that Gaddafi had become an ally of Western imperialism by cooperating with the war on terror and efforts to block African migration to Europe.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECastro2011309-559] Gaddafi was widely perceived as a terrorist, especially in the US and UK.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaynes199058Boyd-Judson200591-560]

### Posthumous assessment

International reactions to Gaddafi's death were divided.[^cite_note-561] Gaddafi was mourned as a hero by many across sub-Saharan Africa but was condemned as a brutal dictator in many Western nations.[^cite_note-562]

After his defeat in the civil war, Gaddafi's system of governance was dismantled and replaced by the interim government of the NTC, which legalized trade unions and freedom of the press.[^cite_note-563] In January 2013, the GNC officially renamed the *Jamahiriyah* as the "State of Libya".[^cite_note-Herald_StateofLibya-564] [Gaddafi loyalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaddafi_loyalism) then founded a new political party, the [Popular Front for the Liberation of Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_Front_for_the_Liberation_of_Libya).[^cite_note-565] Led by Saif al-Islam Gaddafi, the Popular Front was allowed to participate in the [future general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Next_Libyan_general_election).[^cite_note-566]

[^cite_note-6]: &#x20;For purposes of this article, 20 October 2011—the date on which Gaddafi died<sup>[\[1\]](#cite_note-BBC20112-1)</sup>—is considered to be the date he left office. Other dates might have been chosen: On 15 July 2011, at a meeting in Istanbul, more than 30 governments, including the United States, withdrew recognition from Gaddafi's government and recognized the National Transitional Council (NTC) as the legitimate government of Libya.<sup>[\[2\]](#cite_note-INDtncofficialgov-2)</sup> On 23 August 2011, during the [Battle of Tripoli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Tripoli_\(2011\)), Gaddafi lost effective political and military control of Tripoli after his compound was captured by rebel forces.<sup>[\[3\]](#cite_note-liveblog23811-3)</sup> On 25 August 2011, the [Arab League](52625.md) proclaimed the anti-Gaddafi National Transitional Council to be "the legitimate representative of the Libyan state".<sup>[\[4\]](#cite_note-taipeitimmes20110826-4)</sup> On 16 September 2011, the [United Nations General Assembly](31957.md) sat the representatives of the [National Transitional Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Transitional_Council) as Libya's official delegation.<sup>[\[5\]](#cite_note-5)</sup>

[^cite_note-8]: &#x20;English pronunciation: [/ˈmoʊəmɑːr ɡəˈdæfi/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English) [*MOH-ə-mar gə-DAF-ee*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Pronunciation_respelling_key) or [/ɡəˈdɑːfi/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English) [*gə-DAH-fee*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Pronunciation_respelling_key); [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): مُعمّر محمد أبو منيار القذّافي, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Arabic): *Muʿammar Muḥammad ʾAbū Minyār al-Qaḏḏāfī*, [Standard Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_Arabic_language) pronunciation: [\[muˈʕamːar alqaˈðːaːfiː\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Arabic) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/8/86/Ar-Muammar_al-Qaddafi.ogg/Ar-Muammar_al-Qaddafi.ogg.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/8/86/Ar-Muammar_al-Qaddafi.ogg/Ar-Muammar_al-Qaddafi.ogg.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup>, [Libyan Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libyan_Arabic_language) pronunciation: [\[ɡəˈðːaːfi\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA) (eastern dialects) or [\[ɡəˈdːaːfi\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA) (western dialects).<sup>[\[6\]](#cite_note-7)</sup>

[^cite_note-15]: &#x20;Due to the lack of standardization of transcribing written and regionally pronounced Arabic, Gaddafi's name has been [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanized) in various ways. A 1986 column by *[The Straight Dope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Straight_Dope)* lists 32 spellings known from the US [Library of Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Library_of_Congress),<sup>[\[7\]](#cite_note-9)</sup> while [ABC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Broadcasting_Company) identified 112 possible spellings.<sup>[\[8\]](#cite_note-10)</sup> A 2007 interview with Gaddafi's son [Saif al-Islam Gaddafi](1196902.md) confirms that Saif spelled his own name **Qadhafi**<sup>[\[9\]](#cite_note-11)</sup> and the passport of Gaddafi's son Mohammed used the spelling **Gathafi**.<sup>[\[10\]](#cite_note-12)</sup> According to [Google Ngram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Google_Ngram) the variant **Qaddafi** was slightly more widespread, followed by **Qadhafi**, **Gaddafi** and **Gadhafi**.<sup>[\[11\]](#cite_note-13)</sup><sup>[\[12\]](#cite_note-14)</sup> Scientific romanizations of the name are **Qaḏḏāfī** ([DIN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DIN_31635), [Wehr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hans_Wehr_transliteration), [ISO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISO_233)) or (rarely used) **Qadhdhāfī** ([ALA-LC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ALA-LC_romanization))
